NEW SCHOOLS.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE AND THE LOVELY LANE SITE.

Scheme Strongly Criticised.

The question of new schools woo again to the fore at the monthly meeting of the Warringeon Education Cot:mitt.° en Monday evening and an interesting discussion waged round alternative suggestions for the utilisation of the Lovely-lane Bile for educational and recreative purposes. Roberts presided and there were also present :—Aldermen J Smethurst, W Bolton, James Evans, A Bennett, Councillors T Birtles, W H BeMan, C Jackson Holmes J Wilkinson, R Henshall, W .1 Wilson, .1 T Locker, .1 Dickenson. THE BOARD OF EDUCATION AND INDUSTRIA

CENTRES.

The School Management Committee considered extracts from the liolmes-Morant circular, together with communications from the Manchester Education Committee and the Association of Education Com- mittees upon matters arising therefrom, and the follow- ing resolution of the Executive Committee of the Association of Education Committees was approved viz. :—" That it is desirable that a clause be introdneed into the Education (Administrative Provisions (No. 2) Bill providing for the appointment of a Stxtutory Com- mittee representative of local education administra, ion that shall be consulted by the Board of Education before changes are made in departmental ruiee and regula- tions," and it was decided to request the Member of Parliament for the borough to give his support to the proposal when the Eincation (Administrative Pro- visions) (No. 2) Bill is under consideration. Callon STEVIriSoN moved and Mr. I.7esON seconded the approval. Councillor HENSHALL inquired what the exact eigniflcance of the resolution referring to the Holmes- Itforaut circular was.

Canon SrEVEN,OS: replied that it meant that the Bianchester Education Committee felt that the Board of Education ass tLit always in touch with the education of the great cities and towns in tha North— (bear, hear)—and that it would be a good thing if a committee were formed in Lancashire and Yorkshire perhaps which would be able to make representations with regard to the edncational needs and requirements of the great industrial populations which differed in some respects from the populations of other parts of the country. The School Management Committee were in favour of such a Scone.

_ The minutes were agreed to. THE LOVELY-LANE SITE. BOW CAN IT BEM. BE USED?

SUGGESTED APPOINTMENT OF A SCHOOL ARCHITECT.

It having been reported that the General Purposes Committee of the Town Council had not approved of the suggested position of the public elementary school to he erected en the site in Lovely-lane, as shown on the plan submitted to them, the Sites and Buildings Committee resolved, That the General Purposes Committee be asked to allot a sufficient amount of land in the centre of the Lovely-lane site on the side nearest to Lovely-lace for the erection of the Echool thereon." At a later date the committee proceeded to Lovely. lane to view the suggested position of the site for the public elementary school to be erected there, and it wns resolved that the above resolution be rescinded and " That the General Purposes Committee of the Town Council he asked to appropriate to this committee about 2 acres of land en the northern extremity of the site for the purpose of the erection thereon of a public elementary school." The committee carefully considered the question of the appointment of en architect or architects for the +schools about to be built and in the end it wan resolved : "That, in the opinion of this committee, it is desirable that an architect should be employed to carry out the whole of the work in connection with the designing and erection of the schools, and that a sub- committee consinting of Conecillors Hessian, Pemberton, Roberts, and Wilkinson, Mr. R. T. Fair- clongh and the Chairman of the committee (Alderman William Bolton), be appointed to prepare, for the con- sideration of the committee, details of the necessary arrangementa for putting this propossl into effect." Alderman BOLTON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said every endeavour was teirg made to pro vide Echoola aud to mnke progress in the work in-

which the committee wan engaged. Work in connec- tion with the Evelyn-street school was proceeding satisfactorily and they hal let contracts for various fittings and electrical installst ion. An effort was being made to provide a school for defective children in Parr- street. With regard to the Lovely-lane site, the com- mittee originally decided to ask the Town Council to allot land in the centre of the plot for the purposes of a new school. On a subsequent inspection they changed their opinion. Thy now thought the school would be better near the railway. Unfortunately, he wan not present, and no could not advance any of the reasons which were responsible for the decision. As far as he was concerned any pert of the land would suit him, if only they could get the school erected. The com- mittee had nothing to do with the laying out of the whole plot ; their only duty was to provide a school. If they were to have the worse portion of the site they were prepared to take it in preference to having no part at all. The question of the appointment of an architect •Lad been again considered. The committee was now getting so much school property that it was one man's work to see that every- thing was kept in order. More new schools were con- templated end it was thought that if a permanent official were engaged to design and supervise the architectural work at all the schools, it would conduce to greater efficiency and economy. Councillor JACKSON Humes seconded the adoption of the minutes. . . .

--.—.— COnnOilior BELLIAN naked whether the report of the sub-committee with reference to the appointment of an architect would come before the Education Com- mittee before any definite action .s!.as &ma.

Alderman BOLTON : Yea, it will have to come here. Councillor DUMAN : It does not read that way. The minute seems conclusive that the sub-committee shall carry the proposal into effect. If that is so I shall oppose it. Alderman BOLTON gave an assurance that the Educa- tion Committee would have the final veto in the matter and that they were not committed to anything by the appointment of tLe sub-committee. Alderman Beetneer moved that the minutes referring to the allocation of part of the Lovely-lane site for the purposes of a school he referred back. He said he was sorry to disagree with any committee of which he wan a member, hut he always felt bound to act in ' accordance with his own judgment and on this occasion he believed the Chairman of the committee agreed with him. When the original resolution in favour of the erection of the school in the centre of the site was passed, he began to have singular hopes that the com- mittee were beginninh to exercise their imagination and to look at the qiieation in a broad and public- spirited way. He went home feeling quite delighted that they were making some progress in Warrington and gradually coming within the confines of artistic taste and civilization. He was therefore very much undone when, on a second visit of the committee to the site, the earlier and much more sensible recommendaticn was rescinded and they were quietly asked to accept an exceedingly unummetrical and undesirable arraai,ement of the hula. The area of the site, about which there was a long and acrimonious light in the Town Council, was about seven acres and it bad perhaps an great potentiali- ties as any open space in Warrington. The original idea was that theschool should be placed in a aymmetrioal position in the centre of the land, that a portion should be laid out an a bowling green, and that there should be recreation grounds on each side, which would serve the double purpose of playgrounds for the children and open spaces for the public. Anyone with a spark of imagination must feel that a plan of that kind lent itself to very attractive treatment. Up to now, how- ever, the Education Committee never seemed to have considered that aspect of the question. They had been utilitarian to the utmost degree. Imagination had been Milieu dead in their arrangement', and he could not think of a single school--with the exception of the Bement—which had been put in position with any eye to artistic effect or the amenities of the neighbourhood. The alderman continued to put in a strong plea for the erection of public buildings and the laying out of towns on the same lines as had been adopted with such striking effect in continental cities. Their object should be to place buildings in such a position that their beauty would be displayed to the beat advantage and that the tout cnaembiewould be agreeable to the eye and satisfactory to those w3thetic faculties which wee not wholly dead, even in the breasts of the Warrington Town Council. Even from a utilitarian point of view the committee were making a great mistake in putting the Lovely-iane School close to the railway, for it must be disturbing to the work of the school to have trains continually rushing past. He contended that the scheme now recommended for adoption gave them the maximum of inconvenience without& single element of advantage It wes at, absolute waste. The Rev. JAAIES DAVIES seconded the amendment. If the school were placed in the middle of the site, the land on either side could be devoted to public recreation. It could be arranged for the playgrounds to be available after school boars. Mr. CI esoN : How is it that we have this scheme at all ? I gathered from the luke-werm way in which the alderman moved the minutes that he himself does not approve of it. I understand there was a very superior rcbeme to this. Was it rejected by the Town Council and, if so, can any anyone tell on why ? Alderman BOLTON mid the Town Council disapproved of one scheme. The committee then agreed upon another but after a second inspection they submitted the alternative plan now under conaideration. lie did not ace that it mattered much where the school was, so long as they got a school at all. They were making no progress by referring recommendations back time after time.

Mr. J 01.1.1 I" supported the amendment because it would bring into action the scheme which, beyond all question, was the best—namely, that the school should be in the middle of the site, fronting Lovely-lane. No time would be lost by palling the amendment.

FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN.

The State as Over-Parent.

OR. G. H. ROBERTS, AI P, AT WARRINGTON.

A dement' for the provision of meals for necessitous ' school children was made at a public meeting held in the Co-operative Hall on Monday evening under the auspices of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council and the Socialist Society. Mr. Charles Dukes occupied the chair and the principal speaker was Mr. George H. Roberts, M.P. There were also on the platform, Mr. G. F. Robson, Mr. James Hampson, Mr. John Haley, Mr. and Mrs. Littlearood, Mrs. Robson and Mr. E. Readies. There was a good attendance. The CHAIRMAN said that meeting had been convened to demonstrate the need of feeding necessitous school children. The industrial conditions of the present day were such that very few workers were sure of their work and the means necessary for the maintenance of their families. Speaking as a Socialist, he looked forward to the day when children would be fed at school, whether they were children of the workers or not. It was the present chaos In which we liven, that forced the mother front the home to the factory, and the father to be unemployed and have to tramp to seek work.

Mr. Roisooe, the prospective Toides and Labour candidate for Orford Ward, moved the following resolu- tion to the Education Committee. " Tbat this meeting of burgesses and townspeople of Warrington protests against the decision arrived at re the providing of free mesh for necessitous school children and demands that all the children in whose homes the income is less than 2s. 91 per bead per week, after rent is deducted, shall be provided with free meals." Proceeding he said that prior to the Christmas of 19(8-9 it was discovered by those interested in the movement that between GOO and 700 children in War- rington were starving. A resolution was then moved to the effect that they should be provided with free meals by the School Attendance Committee. The Act was put into force in January 1909 and 220 children were fed. This number, it was proved, were the children of parents who were unable to feed them. The children of widows, the neglected children, and the children of those receiving out-relief were not included. If there were 600 or 700 necessitious children in 1008-9 what would there be now when the cost of living had been going up every week? He %vas only appealing to their manhood. He did not ask for charity but for ju,tice. Those children most be fed and at the cost of the rates. Mr. HA m PsoN, the prospective Socialist candidate for St. John's Ward seconded the resoiution and said that it was a source of anguish to him, every time he saw a child insufficiently clad or fed to think they were living in an age of such ideals and education. He was sorry that the Trades and Labour Council were com- pelled to bring forward such a resolution as that rend that night. There was not a more appalling spectacle is this Christian country than to see a child going to school hungry. It was for them to change entirely the system which created that sort of thing. (Applause.) They as parents were responsible for the lives of the children. It seemed to him that members of the Town Council and the representatives on the Education Com- mittee had not fully realised their duties towards the children of the town in so far as there were children going to school hengry, and they had failed to put the Act into operation. There had beea a statement thst there were 35 children being fed, but if there wan ciely one child that had not sufficient food through the poverty or negligence of its parents, it was the duty of every citizen of the town to see that the child was fed. (AVr. iaG. IL ROBF.RTS, IM P., said that he was proud of the fact that he was member of a small party in the House of Commons that introduced a measure for the feeding of necessitous school children in 19f6. He was son.timee called upon to address Sunday religious gatherings, but he had found that la that small Act of Parliament there was more practical Christianity than was to ba encountered in many of the Christian towns of the country. It was smaeing to him that people who pro- fessed o belief in Christianity should sit no quietly when they had a knowledge that there were thousands of children requiring the necessities of life. It was said that the Labour members were undermining the parental responsibility and destroying the home-life of the country, but he (the speaker) could say that degradation was being removed from society by the fact that they were feeding the little ones. No true man or woman could quietly rest with the knowledge that there were children wanting things with which to live and enjoy life. It was the truest economy that the children should be fed. The children of to-day were the citizens of to-morrow, and unless they were given a respectable start in life it could not be expected that the future State would be an efficient one. He advised them to do as had been done is some of the other towns i.e. to appoint an independent committee. In I this way in one town hundreds of children had been found in need of food when the local authority had stated that there were not any. There were many well- meaning people who tried to cover it over by charity, but they did not want charity. (Hear, bear.) Charity was a curse both to those who received it and to those I who gave it. it was not for the children to be fed by charity but as a matter of right. (Applause.) lithe parents ware unable to feed the children

r LET THE STATE STAND AS AN OVER PARENT to them. The labour party bad recognised this and they were seeking to gain it, by making it compulsory for every local education authority to apply the Aot. They would not rest content until they knew that every local education authority was charged with that responsi- bility and could be called to account if it was found that any child had been neglected and the authority had failed t a carry out its duty. It was said that the working classes were getting too restless and dissatis- fied. He knew that they were not in Warrington, but he had aiked himself if the people of Warrington had yet learned to live. Its thooght a great many of them had not fouad how beautiful life might really hr. If they had,they would not tolerate the things thattbey now

did. The sight of starving children and people working their hardest and the fact that education was more sad more wide and thst economic knowledge was made easy of acquisition by the working clissea went to show that poverty was not due to insufficiency but to the fact that the products the wmking chums made were taken to the already well•filled homes instead of lo the homes of the working men. God never made rich and poor—only male and female—and the labour party were asking where were His common gifts going to ? The gifts that should go for the tnlightment and bappinesa of the people went to the landlords. Tt was

CIiARITY WAS SPCRXRD and right and justice demanded. (Applause). The great struggle at the present tine was that capital was claiming that it must bare a large return while labour must be content with the leavings—that the working classes should be compelled to accept a wage that was not a living wage. It svas a positive denial of the right to live. They were out to demand the right to live and they were out to see that the child should be assured of that right. The children ought to live in a state of perfect content and freedom. The happenings of the past few weeks, despite the riots and disorder, went to show that the nation was waking and it was going to be all the better for that waking. (Applause). What they were suffering from was not the lack of wealth but the unfair distribution of it, and it was their manhood tut demanded a fair value for their labour. While the cost of living had increased by ten per ecent. the increase of wages had been one per cent. Th

CHOICE OF WORK.

PROPOSED JUVENILE EMPLOYMENT EXCHANGE.

Trade Unions' Apprentice Restrictions.

There was an interesting discussion et the meeting of the Warrington Education Committee, on Monday on a , proposal to help children just leaving school to obtain !suitable work, by establishing a juvenile employment ' exchange. The Choice of Employment Sub-committee reported : It having been reported that the resolutions of this committee in regard to the draft ncheme in connection with juvenile employment had been withdrawn by the Chairman of the Education Committee at the meeting of the Town Council on the 4tb July, 1911, the com- mittee again considered the scheme, together with a circular letter from the Board of Education stating that the Board would be prepared to make I.ocal Authorities grants in aid of the expenses of the Juvenile Employment Exchanges, the committee decided to re-affirm their previous decis one and resolved, that the provisional scheme drawn up dealing with this matter be approved and ba submitted to the Education Committee for adoption. The scheme deals with children leaving school and young persons up to the age of seventeen. It was also resolved," That, before the foregoing scheme is submitted to the Board of Education for approval, the Divisional Officer of the Board of Trade and a representative of the Board of Education be invited to meet this committee for the purpose of discussing the scheme with them." Mr. UPSON, in moving the confirmation of the minutes, said he understood that the resolutions were withdrawn by the Chairman of the Education Com• mittee at the Council meeting, mainly on the ground that there had been a suspicion that this was a cost that ought to be borne by the taxpayer and not by the ratepayer. Some members of the Town Council thought the work it was proposed tn do might be legitimately done by the labour bureau. In reply to that he wanted to ray that the minutes committed them to no expenditure whatever, the word "pro- visional" had been inserted in the resolution, which wonld prevent any action being taken on the minutes until the scheme had been submitted to the committee and adopted by the Town Council. The tendency of the time and the trend of legislation was to put the control of the children more and more in the bands of the education authorities. They had at the present time bot only the education of the children as mach to consider, but they fed the hungry ones, medically attended the weak ones, and were going to dentally treat them as well. They also insisted upon the children going to ached reasonably well fed and clothed and clean in person and clothing, so that their function was very much wider than that of imparting instruction or giving education. Daring the ago of adolescence they were also beginning to obtain control over the child. They were providing the evening continuation schools, and they hoped in a short time they would have more power still in the direction of seeing that the youth between 13 and 17 should haves fuller and better training for his life's work than he had at present. In all direc- tions there was a tendency for the local education authority to control and direct the life not only of the child but of the adolescent. Now they were giving attention to the employ- , meet of the scholars when they left school. ' That question had created a great deal of interest and ! voluntary associations had been formed to advise parents as to the occupations their children should pursue. That wan widely advocated an a remedy for unemployableness. Beading with the matter in its ! relation to the State, Mr. Upson referred to the dis- cussions chi h bad taken place as to thather the Board of Education or the Board of Trade wee the proper authority to have control of the work. When the Choica of Employment Act (19:0) had been paused a memorandum wee issued to the effect that the local authorities were to advise parents and children in the choice of emplos meat, and the labour exchanges should be responsible for Vs work of registration. Since the Tows Connell discussed the local scheme, the position had been modified by the issue rf • new circular from the Board of Education, providing that part of the expense to which an authority might be put in regal. 1 to seleries, shall be borne by the Board of Education in the making of a grant. Therefore, the question of expense hardly came in at all. Ile gathered

from the valuable report recently prepared by the Director of Education that they had roughly about 8.50. children leaving school every year. and a very large proportion would be found occupations by their parents. A small number, perhaps 30 or 40 per cent, would come under the operations of the committee, and they would be advised, not by an official expressly appointed for the purpose, and who would form Cu additional official in their administrative employ, but the Director said the work could be no allocated among his staff that no additional officer would be required. They would only have to deal with, perhaps, 200 or 300 cases a year. The Board of Education bad given authorities up to Christ- mas, 1911, to put the Act into operation. Possibly after that time the formation of committees would be compulsory. They in Warrington prided themselves upon being a progressive authority and they did not want to be driven to do such a thing. They proposed now to take a step which would have very valuable functions regarding the adolescent in his strugille for existence and in the preparation for his life in the future. Liverpool had already adopted a similar , scheme. I Mr. KING DON reconded.

Councillor BELLIAN said many things in the proposed echeme would be absolutely worthlese and as much of the work and expense was bound to fall upon the Corporation, he should want to know what the cost would be. He agreed as to the desirability of doing something to ensure that children should be placed in suitable situations on leaving school, bat in carrying out the work on the lines proposed there was suns to he over-lapping. The fact that the Board of Educatiom had agreed to meet a portion of the cost showed that his recent opposition was justified. Alderman BOLTON complainef that the Town Council was not adequately represented on the Committee which would be responsible for the working of the scheme. He admired the scheme itself but he feared it was not to be carried out on sufficiently broad lines. Alderman BENNETT agreed with Alderman Bolton'', point as to the representation of the Town Council, but he emphatically endorsed the general principle of the proposals. It was time we introduced some little system in our industrial affairs. We had been blunder- ing along from chaos to chaos too long. and it seemed to him that the whole atmosphere of modern life tended to a scientific readjustment of questions of capital and labour. Councillor PEMBERTON did not see that the scheme would be of 507 practical use. We were suffering from too many fads in education already, and many of the schemes of present-day educationists 'imply led us into a cot-de-soc. The committee could

do everything they pr-to-ph;ed,Yrat—te; could not make a job for a man. In the trades with which be was connected the trade anions determined how many apprentices there should be in a given trade, and for a committee to net out to find jobs would be so absolute waste of public money. He would use all his influence to prevent a single penny being spent on the scheme. What did they want besides the labotir bureau. which was already in existence, with its ofticiars with very little to do? it appeared to be another attempt on the part of the Board of Education to create posi- tions for which the ratepayers would have to pay salaries. The signs of the tirnes were thot thy trade unions were going to dictate still more as to the apprentices they would have in their businesses. They moat have some idea that aq boys could become bank tnauagers, architects ur chemists ; but they would still require somebody to sweep the streets and act as tram-guards. If everybody had a University education they would still require scavengers. (" Th•y would do the work better.") That was not his experience with labourers. He would rather have a man with big muscles to carry bricks up n ladder than a man with a big head and no body. (Laughter). There were plenty of educated kola. (Laughter). He protested against the constant tendency of legislation to force more expense on to municipalities which were already aver-burdened with rates.

IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

THE STRIKE IN IRELAND.

We are not without a certain suspicicn that the trouble in the Irish railway industry this week will be used for political capital, and therefore it may be worth wbile to consider the strike somewhat fully. As a general rule, the strikes of any consequence which have occurred in Ireland have been due to the tidal wave of industrial agitation in England. The unrest to-day, however, finds its storm centre in Ireland itself. When they struck a month ago, the Irish workers were influenced but little by their own grievance; the impelling motive being their close union and sympathy with the English railway workers, who had declared a general strike. In the present instance, the Irish railwaymen themselves have taken the initiative and the outlook from any pointed of view is somewhat dis- turbing. The trouble has its origin in that recent upheaval which dominated the public mind in the feverish holiday season just closed. Following upon the dock strike of June and July last, a series of disputes arose between a number of the timber merchants of Dublin and their employees. These disputes remain unsettled to-day and a large number of timber yards are closed in consequence. The chief point at issue is the vexed and much debated question of recognition of the men's unions, , which has taken the same shape as the analogous Wexford dispute. The masters, in a Gilbertian role of acting in union them- selves whilst repudiating that right on the I men's part, have now come to the point of admitting the men's right to combine, but object to the particular union which the men desire to join. A deadlock arising, certain I of the timber merchants secured outside labour and employed this to bring their consignments to the railway stations. At the Kingsbridge station, the goods men refused to handle the goods of one of these firms. Let it oa noted that this action in striking was not in any way pre-arranged, and there was no authority whatever from the officers of the railway unions. It was one of those spontaneous, undisciplined outbursts which betoken a grim determination on the part of the often ill-requited workers to fight hard and long if need be for a better lot and and a brighter life. As in England, this simmering discontent has overwhelmed the resisting officials of the unions, who, what- ever Lord Claud Hamilton may say or think, are seldom found " leading " for a strike, and realising that they cannot stem the undisciplined outbreak, have wisely enough decided to try and lead it with due restraint. SOME ROOT CAUSES.

The position in Ireland is quietly sugges- tive of some of the evils which lie at the very root of Irish life to-day. If you look all over Ireland at this moment when a strike is progressing towards the imminent starvation state of its capital city, where do you find a mouthpiece of the public interest ? That there is a public interest is vividly manifest in the paralysis of the whole transport system, with the exception of the Dublin and South-Eastern Railway. Were the public interest represented in the government of Ireland, some serious attempt at accommo- dation would have been made long since, before the timber trade dispute had reached the point of interfering with the transit workers. A Home Rule Executive respon- sible to a Parliament of the Irish people could not long have abstained from intervening. But Dublin is a long way from Whitehall and we are not without a sense of the psychology of distance. A trade dispute in a Dublin timber yard scarcely ruffles the surface of the English Departments at Westminster and, as anybody who knows Ireland would instantly agree, Dublin Castle is hardly the place to resort to for industrial conciliation. Another point of interest and suggestion is the re-action of this Irish strike upon the English railways. The English strike, arising similarly out of a local dispute which in turn had its affiliations with the dockers' strike,brought out the Irish workers. Unless the new industrial solidarity is intended to work only one way, it would seem logical to expect the Irish strike to bring out the English workers. Should English rail- ways become involved, the men would be in a strong position; the Dublin timber merchants would probably find a heavier pressure, in view of the enormous interests at stake, brought to bear on them than was experienced by the Liverpool Tramways Committee. We must "wait and see" once more, but those who are interested in the growing need for international brotherhood and infiustrial solidarity, will look on this disconcerting strike of the Irish railway workers with special interest.

CROSS-COUNTRY RUNNING.

The devotees of this clean and healthy sport are preparing for an important season which will be officially opened in this district by the monstre meet of the West Lancashire Association which takes place at tbe headquarters of the Snored Heart Harriers, St. Helens, on October 7th. The Olympic Council bare Included in the list of events to be decided at Stock- tolme, Sweden, in 1912, between July 6th and 15th a cross-country race of about 8,000 metres (approxi- gostely 5 miles) start and finish In the stadium, maximum number of competitors from each nation 12. Indtvidusl and team prizes will be awarded. In the team competition the respective places of the first three men from each side will be added together, and the team having the lowest aggregate number of points will be declared the winner. In the event of a tie the total time taken by the scoring three of each team shell decide." How many locals intend to try for a place in the British team which will provide the chanca to compete for the title of World's champion? The West Lancashire and Liverpool District Associa- tion held their joint annual general meeting at Liver- pool on Thursday last when the following resolution was carried : "'chat the interests of cross-country running in this district be managed in future by the W.L.C.C.A. and that the affairs of the L. a: D.C.C.A be 'merged into the W.L.C.O A." This step was deemed desirable to avoid the expense of running two associa- tions where one should suffice. The following officials were elected for the ensuing season : President, Mr T H Blair (Liverpool Harriers) vice-presidenta, Messrs T Ashton (Sacred Heart), A Bailie (War. ington A.C.), 0 H Boothman (Liverpool Gymnasium), W Cooper (West Cheshire), A G Nicholson (Wallasey A.C.) and W Teylor (St Helios Eteca) ; bon secretary, H N Ellis; hon. treasurer, A Davies; bon auditors, W Collett and H H Herbert. Delegates to the Northern Cross Country Association, Messrs Bailie, Boatsmen, Cooper, Ellis, IL J Lyon and Nicholson. The resignations of Runcorn Holy Trinity and Waterloo Harriers were acsepted and Farnworth were struck off—leaving a membership of 27 clubs. A new code of rules is to be drawn op and preeented together with the balance sheet at a later meeting when the bolding of a novice team race eariy in Dacember will also he considered.

The Warrington Athletic Club will hold their annual general meeting at the Royal Oak Branch on Thursday next at 7.30 p.m, when all members We ',quested to attend, se in addition to the election of officers, head- quarters, Az., the details of the winter and summer piogramme are of vital interest to all athletes within 10 miles of the borough of Warrington.

The West Ridiug Standing Joint Committee has decided to organize a reserve police force of 400 men who will receive a retainer of not more than £5 a year sod pay of from ss. to Sas city for emergency work. At the request of the London Trades Council the local Trades and Labour Council in Manchester again showed their opposition to the Insurance Bill, endors- ing a reaolution from the first-named society asking for he withdrawal of the measure.

NOTES BY " OVALIST "

PAPAKISRA TO PLAY TO-DAY.

Warrington created a sensation in N rthern Union c:rcles when they imported Stantz from Australia, and their enterprise led to a revision of the rules i regarding colonid players. They may be said to have I gone one better this week by signing on '' Dick" Papsknia, a Maori chieftain and one of the finest full- backs in the world. Mr. kshton made somewhat of a stir last Saturday when he appeared on the stind with the dark-skinned athletic looking Maori, but only preliminary negotiations had been completed then. The player was willing, but Warrington hive since obttined permission t play 1:1;in by cabling the New Zealand Union. Papakura will certainly turn out to day at Wilderspool, taking Fletcher's place at full- back an I probtbly having a trial also at the centre p

The famous Maori footballer has not been imparted direct from New Zealand, but only from the Crystal Palace, where he was one of the leading figures in the Maori village. fie male a great reputation in Australasia as fu I back, and captained the Maori team which toured Australia with great success during 1910 and also played against tne N.C. tAiring team in 1910. Men like Shusars and Johnston, who have seen him play, spe k -highly of Papakura's skill and vigour. He is a finely-built man with exceptional muscu'ar development and a solid weight of II stones for his five feet ten inches.

Con hardly be expected that Papakura will be in first-class form until he has had some practice, but as • teetotaller and non-smoker he is a man always in the condition known as '' fit ' and should soon be at home again in the football field.

None of my readers must jump to the conclusion that Papakura is an uneducated native. On the contrary he is a well-educated man, an excellent conversationalist and a capital writer. I hope and expect that he sill be a credit to the team in all res ,ects.

Papakura will have the di•tinetion of being the first native foitballer to play in England under Northern Union rules.

I had a chat with Papakara yesterday afternoon, just after he had signed the various papers necessary, for becoming a Northern League player, and found him lo.ating forward with keen inteiest to donning the jersey again. In reply to a question as to fitness, he said he was a 'bit heavy, and would take a week or two to get into condition. He was thankful it was only a "light" match to-day, though no one enjoyed a keen game more, he said.

Papakara said he played five times against the English N U. team in 1910, twice at Sydney and three times in New Zealand. Be confirmed, with an amused smile, the narrative that in one match he scored a try from frel back, and completed the feat by jumping over W. Batten's head.

As usual, the contest between Warrington and Hull was full of good football and afforded much satisfac- tion to a large crowd. Warrington had a difficult task to defeat the Third Port-mm, and when the visitors got an early lead of five p ,ints there were not many people who would have laid guineas to gooseberries on Warrington's ultimate success. The locals how- ever, showed more grit than they did at St. Helens, and after levelling matters up by half-time, they final'y got the lead and secured full points in an important match.

On the whole, I think Hull were rather a smarter team than Warrington, but their one weak spot happened to be Warrington's strength and this turned the scale in favour of the Wirepullere. It was at half- back where Warrington gained the day. Brooks was iu magnificent form and finding a weak pair opposed to him, he made full use of his weight and p ice. The way he broke away from the pack and raced clear has ryit been seen since the season of the Kangaroos' last visit, when the Warringtn man gained international honours and wad considered the best half in the Union. It is a happy co-incidence that the arrival of the second Australian team finds Brooks once more in form. The marvel is that he was so much inferior dating the whole of last season and never once gave a display approaching that on Saturday, or the one against Leigh.

Wsrrington's great nee' is, as everybody is aware, a class threequarter hoe. Woodburn is the only , player in the present quartette who is really up to standard, And one misses the fast running and smart combination of the Warrington threequart-rs of old With the Hull threequarters behind the Warrington halves on Saturday there would have been tries galore. As it was it took Warrinton all their time to win, and the result was largely due to the single- handed tuns of Brooks, and his tricky partner, Matt Creevey. Warrington's scoring power is about half what it ought to be, and until we get threequarters of 1, the right kind it will not improve. There are possi- bilities about a line composed of Woodburn, Frawley, Papakura and Waywell, if we can only get it ! The Warrington forwards did some fine, bustling work in the loose last Saturday, but they failed again and again to get the ball out of the scram smartly. Then: is something radically wrong with the scrummage work, and in each match this season the opponents have had possession about twice as often as the Wirepullers. The only remedy, it seems to me, is to graduilly draft new blood into the forward division until we get six men who give satisfaction. There are obviously one or two men in the Warrington pack who do not like geiting their heads down and " grafting." Time will weed diem out, especially as there are some promising youngsters coaling on !

By the way, what bas come over Johnston ? Be was in very poor form last Saturday. Another man who has disappointed so far is Skelltora.

A word of praise is left for Tilley, who again gave a fine display on the defence.There were anxious moments occasionally when the speedy Hull wings came tearing down the field, but the veteran was never found wanting. Lk of speed prevents him playing the modern game, but on the defence Tilley takes some beating. Clarksol struck me as having deteriorated eince he left Leigh, who certainly have a much finer back in Dolewski. Mclntyre was among the spectators

on Saturday. Tbis is not the proper place for a young player with ability and ambitition !

Warrington have a very busy week end before them and I am none too sanguine about the results of the visits to Barrow today, and Huddersfield on Monday. ' We loot both matches last season, and the relative strength of the teams has not altered much. A victory on Barrow soil is not too much to ho pe for, and it would be very encouraging for the small band of enthusiasts who propose to go to Huddersfield on Monday.

The team for to-day is :—Tilley, full bock ; Woodburn, Unsworth, taylor and Bradshaw, three- quarters; Broke and Creevey, hslf-backs ; Johnston, Shugars, Thomas, Chester., Naylor and Bsocklehnrst, forwards. It will bs noted that one of the most promii,ing of the young forwards is promoted, and that Taylor gets the preference over Dickenson.

The "A " team will be :—Fletcher, full back ; Clare, Carter, Critchley and Shakeshaft, three- quarters ; Lloyd and Daintith. half backs; Arnold Trainer, Bradshaw, Harmer, Green and Skelhom, forwards. Some interest will be taker) in the appear- ance of Arnold (who has been ill) and Tranter, a well- built junior from P. A K. who has just signed on. Warrington Winning Again. Hull Defeatad After a Fine Game.

The visit of the Hull team to Warrington is always loolked forward to with interest, and there wa4, comes nuently, a capital crowd of 7,000 at Wilderspool on Saturday. Beautiful weather again prevailed and a fine, fast game was witnessed between well-matched teams. Both aides fielded their chosen thirteens, nod the teams and officials lined out as folio as :

- back ; H Brachhaw, R Unswortb, 0 Dickenson, and W Woodburn, three. quarter backs ; fil Creevey and E Brookes, half backs ; M Johnston, F Shuns, J W Cheaters, A Skelhoro, A Naylor, and G Thomas, forwards. Hull—E Clarkson, back ; A Francis, J Devereux, Rogers and E Rogers, three-quarter backs ; H Wallace and F Battersby, half backs ;0 Connell, T Herridge, A Allen, It Tailor F Boyle. and W Holden, forwards. . .

Referee, Mr Ci F Dickenson' Halifax; touch judges, Messrs W Yates, Runcorn, an,i W Johnson, Salford. Warrington wee the toss, and with wind and eon behind them they soon forced the Hull men to their own quarter. Brooks and Creevey got the ball out smartly, and ilradshaw mode a praiseworthy dash down the touch-line but was well tackled at the corner by E. Rogers. Keen scrummaging closely followed, and dohuton tried to force his way over but was held. Francis changed the scene in a twinkling by snapping up a Warrington pass and racing away to centre where Creevey and Woodburn brought him down. Hull now seemed to find their feet and smart play by the hacks harrassed the home defence. Devereaux and Francis got away at a fine speed, and after exchange passing the centre shok off a tackle by Dickensan and grounded near the pasts, for E. Rogers to add an easy goal. Tale was a serious set-back for the locals, wha at ome set about reduciag the lead. Fine work in the loose by Cheaters, Naylor, and Skelhorn resulted in the capture of Devereaux in his own quarter, and a hot attack was set up. Brooksand Creevey tried hard to get through, and Johnston buttered a promising pass. Hull gradually worked back to centre, but Brooks at last got away with a dashing run. The Hull halves failed to stop him, and when near the line Brooks parted to Creevey who drew Clarkson and then threw across to Woodburn. The latter secured smartly and dashed over at the corner, but f riled to adds goal. Warrington kept up a lively pace and Clarkson was grassed by Woodburn who robbed him of the ball and bad ruu round the pouts as the whistle sounded for a penalty against Hull. This WU unlucky for Warrington, but they were having the better of the game and when 0 Rogers tried to clear with a weak pant, Tilley replied with a flue drop from centre which sent the ball over the bar and equalised the scores. Half-time War- rington, 1 goal 1 try (5 points) ; Hull, 1 goal I try (5 points). The resumption band Warrington still in deters mined mood and a great run by Brooks nearly resulted in another try. Clarksoa cleared to centre but there was no holding the Warrington halves, sod Brooks going away again with rare dash sent C ) over at the corner with a grand try, which ' Thomas could not improve. Hull cautioned to defend, but after Unswortb had been well checked, the visitors made headway. Twice E. Rogers got away and raced half the length of toe field but Tilley tackled well and the home line was held este. Hull were aggressive, but the Warrington defence was excellent and Dickinson finally cleared the invaders out. Bradshaw and Thomas got away finely on the right and ran down to Clarkson, but the wing man was adjudged forward when a certain score was imminent. There was little of further incideat iu the game, Warrington having rather the best of matters, and being largely helped by Tilley's fine punting in the rear. WARRINGTON'S CRICKET SEASON.

It is many years since cricketers have beenl favoured with such an ideal minion as the one to which they have just reluctantly bidden good-bye. The Warrington Club took full advantage of the remark- able succession of brilliantly fine Saturdays, and although the season was not conspicuous for great achievements, some highly interesting cricket was seen and the record, on the whole, does credit to the club. It must be borne in mind that the batting department was considerably weakened by T. Wtight's transference of allegiance to Earlestown at a comparatively early stage and another severe handicap was imposed by the indisposition and consequent absence from numerous matches of the Rev. Horace Gray. The highest individual score of the season falls to the credit of W. Richardson who at Stockport made the splendid not out innings of 123 That match was also notable for Warrington's best batting record-259 for the loss of seven wickets. The hest batting average is secured by J. W. Pearson who has shown consistently good form, and one of chief events was J. Callon's sparkling exhibition against Ashton-under-Lyne, his 117 being free from blemish. Youthful talent has been well to the fore, G. Woodcock, a promising Botelerian, distinguishing himself with a masterly innings of ti.lagainst Blackley although he fell off a little towards the close of the' season and was log before wicket thrice in succession. Stanley Partidge did splendid work with bat and ball. The bowlers had not much opportunity ou an excep- tionally dry wicket, but Pestell and W Bennett, who shared the burden of the wok, did well under trying conditions. Mr. Silcock together with his deputy, IR. W. Richardson, have ably led the team and Duckworth has many titian kept wicket in capital style. THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 23,1911

T he Parliamentary THE INSURANCE adjournment will at SCHEME UNDER least insure that the FIRE. National Insurance Bill will be discussed from every possible point of view before it finally passes through Committee in the House of Commons. Among other bodies which have recently had it under considera- tion are the Association of Chambers of Commerce, the Steel Company of Scotland, the North British Locomotive Company, and the Trade Union Congress. At the Dublin Conference of the Chambers of Commerce a resolution was passed expressing the view that the heavy burden which the Bill proposes to place upon production must eventually adversely affect the working classes. It was further urged that the provisions in the second part of the Bill would relieve merely the evils of unemploy- ment without removing the cause thereof, and would tend to increase unemployment, rather than to diminish it. At the annual meeting of the Steel Company of Scotland the Chairman described the Bill as crude, ill-considered, and unjust, and said that the payment by the company of threepence a week per workman for sickness insurance would be equal to an additional income tax of 7d. in the XI. The company now pays an income tax of is. 2d. and the directors estimate that if the Bill passes they will have to pay is. 9d., or half as much again. In the case of the North British Locomotive Company—which employs many more men in proportion to its profits that the steel company does— it is estimated that the payment of the employers' weekly contribution towards sickness insurance would come to no less than 3s. in the on the profits of the company. Other cases were cited at the Chambers of Commerce conference in which the employers' payments under the scheme would represent an even higher proportion of profits. These are formidable estimates, though it may be pointed out that from some points of view it would be fairer to give the percentage of increase which sickness insurance would add to the wage-sheet of the year. It may also be recalled that similar estimates were given at the time of the passing of Mr. CHAMBER- LAiN's Workmen's Compensation Act. But notwithstanding this extra charge on the manufacturing interest there has been a steady increase iu production, and it may be assumed either that prices have been increased or that economies have been effected in other directions. Mr. LORIMER, indeed, in his address to the shareholders of the Steel Company of Scotland, admitted the possibility of meeting the extra financial burden by increasing the price of their pro- ducts, but said that in this case the ship- builders' engineers, boiler-makers, etc.,would undoubtedly 'pass on the charge as ar as they could to their buyers. But .` this plan" in his view "violates an essential principle of sound finance, for every transfer of the charge would, wherever possible, be loaded with a profit thereon, so that the consumer would pay far more than ever reached the Treasury." It is. however rather late in the day to rely on this argu- ment, for free education, workmen's com- pensation, and old age pensions have all added to the charges upon business under- takings, though they have probably been partly met by the cheapening of production through the introduction of labour-saving machinery. It can scarcely be doubted, however, that the effect of social reform legislation will be to increase prices, and in a certain degree to add to the cost of living. We are aware that this is denied by some authorities, who contend that with better industrial conditions a higher level of efficiency will be reached, but experience shows in the most progressive nations that there is an upward tendency in price., which may reasonably be attributed in part to the cost of the improved conditions insisted

upon by the State. Sooner or later however, an addition to the cost of living renders an increase of wages necessary, and in this way there is a relative economic adjustment. But the figures we have quoted, as well as the estimates of the CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER, plainly show that the proposal made at the Trade Unions Congress in favour of a non-contributory scheme was hopelessly impracticable, and we are glad to see that it was rejected. The more modest proposal that workers with a wage of less than 15s. per week should be altogether relieved of contributions was carried by a majority vote, but there are strong objections to the non- contributory principle, for the door would be opened to constant demands for fresh conces- sions, which it would be difficult to resist if the non-contributory principle were admitted. The problem, however, of the poorest class of workers is a very difficult one, for on the one hand they stand most in need of sickness insurance, and on the other they can ill spare, the weekly pence required under the Govern- ment scheme.

There is a familiar say- MR. HAROLD SMITH, ing in Warrington M.P., IN A TANGLE. which consists of only two words "ware wire I "—but they have a world of meaning in them. Apparently Mr. Harold Smith was ignorant of the phrase or he would have thought twice before stating publicly that if the Corporation wanted a quantity of wire, and the price was £3,000 in Warrington and £2,750 in Germany, they should buy it in Warrington, to- encourage local industries. As a platform statement at an open-air meeting it went down well, because there was no necessity to support this new theory of business or " protection "by reason or argument. But, first Mr. Pearce Williams, and then Mr. Osborne tried to get the Member to discuss the proposition in the columns of the press— to no avail! Mr. Smith has shirked the issue and positively will not enlighten us further as to his new school of economics, or his new theory in business. Even Tariff Reformers advocate buying in the cheapest market as a rule, but Mr. Smith is an exception. He would spend Warrington money in buying Warrington wire, even if wire were being given away in Wigan or St. Helens or Germany. But when he had made the good old firm of Warrington Ltd. bank- rupt and his friends the wire manufacturers millionaires, what would he tell the Official Receiver?

"Sub Rosa" of the THE SALT OF THE "Morning Leader," who EARTH—IN THE in political life is Mr. SCHOOLS. Spencer Leigh Hughes, Liberal M.P., for Stock- port, has been amusing himself with discuss- ing the contents of various Parish Magazines. It is (he says) like a Quiet Day with the Clergy just to peep within their severely simple covers—everything is so far removed from strife and clamor and party prejudice." Then be turns to the "Wheelock Parish Magazine," and remarks that "the Vicar (the Rev. Reginald J. Milward) is booming his own church schools in the " only-genuine- loeware-of-spurious-imitations " vein, and writes thus : Church schools are the salt of the country, which keep gill the other schools from going bad. Happily in Cheshite they are a good big lump of salt, for I find that (,utaide the county boroughs Chester, Birkenhead, Crewe, Stockport, Maccles- field, Warrington) there are (roughly), 33,500 thildren in Church schools, 18,000 in conaeil schools, and 5,000 in other schools. Now we mean to hold on to our lump of salt. "Salt is good " and Cheshire produces plenty of h. . . There is no guarantee that religion will be properly taught in any except our religious schools. Of course, the vicar knows that there is a Wesleyan school at Wheelock in which the excellent syllabus of religious education adopted by the Cheshire Education Com- mittee is taught, but he still holds that his school prevents the Wesleyan school from "going bad." I hope all Wesleyan readers will take note that there is "no guarantee" that religion is properly taught in their schools and that they are indebted to the vicar's" lump of salt." Probably they will -take this clericalist tale with a pinch of salt."

"The World," one of

THE PARTY the leading sixpenny WITHOUT A POLICY, weeklies on the Tory side, has no illusions in regard to the moribund condition of the Tory Party, and week by week it is striving to formulate a policy designed to lead Toryism out of the wood and into power again. The suggestions frequently border on the desperate, but then this friend of the old regime is nothing if not candid and fully realises that no half measures will pull Toryism out of the mire. " But what a misfortune for the Conservative Party if office were thrust tit:rut it to-morrow ! With no decided leadership, lacking any impera- tive policy, how long would inch a party be likely to continue in plwer ? "

Really, we are almost sorry that Mr. Balfour or Mr. F. E. Smith is not asked to form a cabinet—the effort would be so divert- ing. But "The World" is extremely serious, and after looking about cautiously it suggests the revival of the smallholder in industry and agriculture, as a deliberate challenge to the tendency of trustification and nationalisation. Toryism must begin to truckle to the democracy, and find means of tickling the groundlings. What are principles?—no use in politics from the worldly " World " point of view. Every- body must own something, and he must get it from the Tory Party. The trap must be well-baited, for "Broadly speaking, the Liberals legislate for the benefit of the consumer, the Tories for the benefit of the producer. As long as England is the Carter Paterson of the world, and as long as the producer is largely a hand in a factory, so long will legislation for the producer be nnpopul Li- and, indeed, unsound. The reviv,il of the small holder is the only means of making legislation in the interests of the producer both sound and popular. As I have already hated, the small owner is fundamentally a Conservative. In a nation of small holders the Liberals would never be in power." There you have the new policy of the old party in a nutshell. It is one of expediency pure and simple. Toryism has to be care- fully sugar-coated to make it acceptable to the masses. When they have swallowed it they will soon be looking anxiously for the present-day equivalent of the old Roman vomitarium. But to get them to swallow is all "The World" thinks about. And the writer's precious policy is entitled "The Revival of England." We shall look fordrard to the next chapter!

A Pontoetyydd farmer on Thursday found his wife, aged 91, lying dead on the mountain between Pontypool and Abertillery. She had gone to fetch water from a spring on the previous night, when a thunderstorm broke over the district, will she had been struck by lightning. Parts of the woman's clothing were found 35 yards away.

WW NOTES AND NOTIOrt]

Judging from a letter in our correspond- ence columns the local p3lice might spend a profitable afternoon in bringing to book some of the " sportsmen " who indulge in the brutal sport of rabbit-coursing in the neigh- bourhood of Longford or Winwick.

• • An interesting passenger by the White Star steamer Cymric, which left the Mersey for Boston on Tuesday evening, was Mr. A. Tennyson Dickens, eldest surviving son of the famous novelist, whose primary object in visiting the States is to deliver a series of lectures there during the next twelve months.

* - Sir A. Conan Doyle, asked by an Irish newspaper for a message to " Unionists" for to-day's meetings in Belfast replies that he has twice been a Conservative can- didate, but is now strongly in favour of Home Rule. It will probably be found, when the struggle comes, that many "Unionists" have revised their views in regard to granting Home Rule to Ireland.

* The date for the pile-driving ceremony for the new Warrington Bridge has, at last, been fixed. Only the Mayor and Alderman Smethurst are to have the honour of driving piles—a decision which will be a source of disappointment to at least one other Alderman. We think the Deputy-Mayer might have been included, seeing that he applied himself earnestly to the forwarding of the bridge scheme during the time he occupied the mayoral chair. *

The official year book of the Wesleyan Methodist Church, issued this month, shows that the present number of ministers is 3,615; lay preachers, 31,150; church members, including probationers, 817,409; Sunday schools, 10,678 ; officers and teachers, 144,364; and scholars, 1,160,569. The number of Wesleyan churches in Great Britian is now 8.668, with seating accommoda- tion for 2,323,498 persons.

In the" Review of Reviews" Mr. W. T. Stead says that "The Parliament Bill, insisting as it doss upon three sessions and two years being spent on any Bill which is to evade the Peers' veto is a very worthless measure from the Radical point of view. Its chief value lies in the statutory affirmation of the exclusive right of the Commons over money bills. The nonsense that the Tories have talked about the destruction of the House of Lords wi.l soon be recognised at its true value. Resurrection rather than destruction would be the fitter term to apply." ,

Still another way of disposing of the obnoxious £4OO per year which some M.P.'s are inclined, quite erroneously, to think is baing forced upon them! Mr. R. A. Yerburgh, M.P. for Chester, one of the wealthiest members of the Tory party, has promised his £4OO to the Navy League. Our own member has not yet followed any of the glowing examples of self-sacrifice set by fellow M.P.'s, or given any indication of the way in which he means to reconcile his conscience with the acceptance of a salary from the Liberal Government. He hiss not even said "Thank you!"

0 The members of the Town Council will meet on Monday evening next for the pur- pose of selecting the Mayor for the ensuing year. So far as can be ascertained, the general feeling is in favour of inviting Coun- cillor Dr. Joseph to accept office for another year, and we are sure such a course would meet with the approval of the townspeople who have fully appreciated the thoroughness and devotion with which his Worship has attended to the manifold duties of his office. * * *

- A story is going the rounds which illustrates the "offishness " still to be found in some churches and chapels. A man sat through the service one Sunday morning wearing his bat. When requested to do so he removed the hat smilingly. He was afterwards asked if he had worn the hat purposely or if it was merely absent-minded negligence. "No," said the man. "I have been attending this church regularly for nearly two years, and no one hasever spoken to me in all that time. I just thought I would leave my hat on my head this morning to see if it would serve as an introJuction to someone. lam glad to meet you." * * *

The Warrington Education Committee has shown its desire to keep abreast of the times by provisionally adopting a scheme to assist children leaving the various schools of the borough in choice of suitable employment. There is a consensus of opinion that the unemployed and unem- ployable classes have been greatly aug- mented in the past by the haphazard way in which children have been allowed to drift into situations, regardless of their suit- ability or of their prospects in life. The juvenile employment exchange will, without trespassing upon the rights of the parent, deal with a difficult question on sympathetic and systematic lines and should do a great deal to avoid that placing of" square pegs in round holes " which is in many cases fraught with lamentable results.

Warrington has ihis-week had a visit from an interesting " globe-trotter " in the person of George M. Schilling, a plucky American who is engaged in his second walk round the world. Wherever he travels he wheels a large hollow globe which on occa- sion serves as his lodging for the night. Mr. Schilling has had a life of adventure and has undertaken many long-distance walks, one of his earliest being from Pittsburg to San Francisco and back—a distance of 8,900 miles in nine months and twenty-eight days.lt was in 1897 that he first essayed the gigantic task of walking round the world. He set off without a cent in his possession paying his way by selling postcards and books. Mr. Schilling is undertaking his present walk on similar lines and he has interested curious audiences of Warringtonians by his vivid accounts of his exciting experiences in many lands.

There was a pissage in the leading columns of Monday's " Morning Post " which may serve to allay the fears of those timorous folk who are convinced that under a Radical administration the defensive services of the country have been so starved and mismanaged that we are entirely unrrepared for war. "These," says the "Post ' at the end of a long article on the manccuvres, "are the reasons why the British Army in 1911 is in much better coniition than it was, say, in 1903. Its generals and staffs have been working hard to master their business, and have had for some years past the practice that is indis- pensable for them. For the same reasons the Territorial Army is a much better organism than was the Volunteer Force from which it proceeded." The credit for that improve- ment has to be distributed among men of all ranks, from Lord Haldane downwards. The Secretary of State for War would be the last

man to claim more than is his due. But such au unbiassed testimonial to the results of his period of office is a valuable antidote to the attempts which have been made in some quarters to cry down the Army for the sake of attacking the Ministry.

S ° * Sir Walter Nuge. t, Nationalist member for South Westmeath, who addressed a United Irish League meeting in Manchester on Saturday, pointed out that the granting of Home Rule would tend to strengthen rather than to weaken the bond between England and Ireland, and that with the increased taxable capacity which would ultimately result from the prosperity of the country Ireland would become a valuable asset to the Empire. The majo ity of Irish Unionists, he said, were secretly as anxious as the Nationalists for the establishment of a Parliament in Dublin. •• . .

Lori Claud Hamilton, chairman of the Great Eastern Railway Company, was the only witness examined before the Railway Settlement Commission on Monday. He attributed all the upset among railwaymen to the interference of trade union officials, and declared himself utterly opposed to "recognition." He said he regretted the intervention of the Board of Trade in the recent dispute, and regarded the agreement arrived at as inopportune, seeing that in another four days the whole strike move- ment would have been broken. ,

*5 * ' In the course of examination by Mr Henderson. M.P., Lord Claud Hamilton instanced the glass trade as one which had been driven out of England by the arbitrary conduct of the trades unions. Our old friend "Workman Jim" has always contended that the glass trade was ruined for the want of protective tariffs. Here we have a distinct difference of opinion on a question of fact between two authorities on the same side. Are we to believe Lord Claud Hamilton or " Workman Jim " ? Or can it be that both are wrong? When doctors disagree the sick man wins back to health. There must surely be a chance for the glass trade !

No ru; is more poPular ;ith u—s than the one to Davenham, and so 19 set out on Saturday, the 13th,1 and returned, as luck would have it, without a hitch.l One gentleman had a puncture at Stretton and, on setting off again, the four very amateur repairers left his lamp on the hedge bottom. Why don't cyclists, instead of po.tting the lamp down anywhere near, slide it on the lamp bracket again? It will fit both aides up, and if it should be forgotten, in the hurry of catching up the others, it won't be forgotten lone, wrong side up, and in any case it won't be left behind. A short detour was taken through Gorstage, in order to avoid the awkward village of Weaverham, and by 5 o'clock we were discuzsing a well-served tea in the garden at Mrs. Booth's Cafe next the schoo's. After a short and happy walk round by Beehive Farm, happy through the antics of the comic members of the party in their unrehearsed private theatricals, the return journey through Lostsck and Budworth commenced, also happily. It nearly finished on London Bridge, Stockton Beath, where the police had something to say in the motter of " No lamp," but we will draw a veilover what followed. Distance 2: miles. Warrington Caledonian Association On Thursday evening the Warrington Caledonian Association opened it 4 winter session by bolding a smoking concert at the Lion Hotel. There was a fair attendance of members and friends, the chair being occupied by Mr. J. L. Davenport, the popular and enthusiastic vice-president of the association. The programme opened with selections on the pianoforte by Mr. J. H. Forster, whoa's) ably accompanied the various singers during the evening. Mr. J. B. Poole next favoured with a s.mg " Where bright glances are." Later he contributed " Tbe Cradle Song " and in response to a most hearty encore gave " The pale young Curate." Mr. Davenport sang the old Scots' favourite " My amn Folk," and also joined Mr. Poole in a fine rendering of the duet " Watchman, what of the night ?" The following also contributed songs during the evening, viz. :—Messrs. J. Hunter, J. Bennett, J. Mc'Dermid, P. Gillespie, J. Sdrling, G. Marshall and A. Love. The contribritiQns were mainly old, well-known Frottish sorgs, the singing of which was received with every mark of appreciation by the assembled Caledonians. At the conclueion of the programme a cordial vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Davenport both for his conduct in the chair, and for bin end-vices in arranging the smoker. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Even the nrat fastidious cannot but appreciate the excellent programme presented at this popular place of entertainment this week. The items are of a most varied and original type while the visit of Fred Karno's celebrated comedians, who always attract hip audiences, ban agaiu proved no exception to the rule, crowded houses being the order for the whole week. The two productions "Mr. Justice Perkins and The Bailiff" are led by that able actir Mr, Albert Bruno and assisted by a large company his adventures in the role of Perkins provoke endless amusement. "Me. Justice Perkins" is an act in three scenes which depict the selection of Perkins as a "justice," a scene in the corridor of the court in which Perkins arrivrs arrayed in shooting attire to take up his past on the Bench ,and fiaa'ly the scene in the court interior. His inability for this position can be readily imagined by the fact of his dismissal of a bookmaker on the promise of a good tip "and by his repeated calls for lionar and the 1 k test sportiog papers while the trial of his own case for breach of promise in which he departs to slumber during the hearing of the ' prosecution and afterwaids compels the jury to award the complainant a farthing damages, at the point of the pistol, are only some of the events which teed to make this prriuction such a pronounced success. The Bailiff " is also composed of three scenes which deal with the engagement of Perkins ass bailiff, and the characters employed to" gain possessioa " .at which he is a pronounced failure) and lastly the dia.- cavery that the wrong house has been sold. One of the most clever and original gymnastic ac's seen here is provided by the Five Petleys (four gentlemen and one lady) whose aerial coatortions and balancing earn warm approval. Will H. Fox presents a number of comely trick piaao p'aying acts while the Cedrics in an original cnnedy act and Mimic, Mine singer and step-dancer are seen to advantage. SPECIAL POLICE RESERVE.

Troops to be Replaced by Civilians.

The Mayor of Warrington has this week receivtd an important circular which the Home Secretary has addressed to chief magistrates throughout the country, urging police authorities to take immediate steps to build up adequate police reserves which will enable them to cope with future troubles without laving recourse to military aid. The Home Sectetary points out that, although the immediate need for the appointment of special constables has passed. it is of great importance that the steps which have been taken for the registration of suitable persons ready to to serve as special constables should be continued, and that there should be in every police district a classified register of persons whose services would be available in any serious emergency. Mr. Churchill suggests the famation of a First Police Reserve, consisting of men accustomed to discipline and trained in the police or army, and a Second Police Reserve of special constables, and says it may sometimes be perfectly legitimate to enrol strikers as special constables, as the disturbances accompanying , strikes are usually caused by " hooligans " having no connection with the strikes. _ .

We are informed that the Watch Committee of the Warrington Corporation will at an estly meeting consider what steps shall be taken to give effect to the Home Secretary's rec unmendalions locally. Already the police have enrolled a number of volunteers and lists of those who are willing to serve in the vari, us classes of reservists will shortly be prepared. The Chief Consiabie would be glad to hear from any townspeople who are desirous of registering them- selves for this important branch of public service.

The Liberal Committee of the Whit,eross Ward met in Thewlis-street Schools on Thursday evening to mske arrangements for the November election. Alderman Et 0;3 and Councillor 111. w-rums spoke eulogistically of the :ervices rendered by Councillor Roberts during his term of office in the Town Council and Councillor ROBERT, replied and referred to various improvements necessary for the ward. The following resolution was unanimously adopted: That this meeting desires to record its warm appreciation of the services of Councilior Henry Roberts as the repreysotative of this ward, cordially invites him to accept nomination again, and pledges itself in the event of a contest to use every legitimate means to secure his election by a large majority." MUZZLED.

A Liverpool Comment on Warrington

Alderman's Action.

An incident has happened in Warrington !says the

" Liverp3ol Toddy Post and Mercury") which may be studied with symplthetic interest in Liverpool. At the Revisi. n Court, objection was made to the vote of a certain local alderman, and when the evidence showed that he lacked the necessary qualification as a burgess he was struck cff the roll. It has always been supposed that an accident cf this kind involved the resignation of his seat by an alderman or councillor. Elderly persons in Liverpcol will recollect the famous case of Mr. William Sir:ups:in. of the Linding-stage, ' who, being returned as a Liberal councillor, was objected to by the Conservatives on the ground that ' the Landing-stage was not situated within the borough boundaties, and as this view was upheld by authorative topographers. Mr. Simpson lost his vote, and promptly resigned his seat, under the impression that if he did not, be would render himself liable to all move of pain. and penalties. It seems, however, that Mr Simpson and his advisers were under a mistake, and that the loss of vote does not necessarily invelve loss of seat. The Warrington alderman declines to be evicted. He says that as he is not , n the burgess roll be is not qualified to speak or vote, but that he is not disqualified from being an alderman. So an alder- man he remains, and an alderman who will never interfere even in the most exciting discussion, and who will perforce remain mum when the card is

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Facing the World Alone."

The authors of " Facing the world alone " have (veered clear of the defects which are too often unpleasantly evident in modern melodrama and have given no a play of genuine absorbing interest. While no undue tax is imposed upon the credibility of the audience, there is sufficient of the sensational and exciting to k:ep even the seasoned playgoer on tenter-hooks, and a great recommendation is the absence of irrelevant " padding " Skilful use has been made of the promising material afforded by the story of an heiress who in childhood is spirited away from a luxurious home to be brought up as a London flower girl. After years of unremitting inquiry her mother learns of her whereabouts, but the restoration of the kidnapped one to her proper social sphere comes as an alarming surprise to Captain the Hon. Reginald Parkhurst, who stands next in succession to the estates. He at ones sets to work t,) get id of this very serious tar to his worldly prosperity, first concocting an ingenious plot to show that the girl is an imposer and next attempting to murder her. Of course, all his schemes are thwarted in the end and the heroine's cu., of bitterness is once more transformed into unalloyed First in a very capable company comes Miss Ethel Van Praagh whose ortrayal of Melia Burgess, the flower girl, wins the vmpathy and approval of the audi, ace. She realizes tLe varying morals of the part with remarkable success. and at one moment has the "louse" alm,st in team whilst at the next thsy are laughing at lisr Cockney witticisms and Ictoyancy of spirit. Mr. J. Edward Whiny is calmly convincing an Captain Parkhurst,tbe evil genius of the piece. He does not succumb to the temptation which besets stage villains to oter-do their parts, but is the more effective on that account. Mr_ Rob, rt S. Pittard is well placed in the role of Caleb Valentine, the ardeut lawyer lover, and contrives to introduce acceptable touches of merriment, in pleasing contrast to the virtionsly mournful air often assumed by heves. Mr. Lim Corless gives a fine study of that old reprobate, James Sudbury ; Miss Hors Hastings is a tender and affectionate Lady Communise: and Mies May Neilson adds much to the success of the production by Ler lively acting as Flash Nance Petro/. The mounting' f the pay is on a lavish scale. Mr. Bootbrovis orchestra submits a nicely varied pro- gramme in creditable style.
