ADOPTED CHILD'S DEATH. IMPORTANT PROSECUTION BY THE GUARDIANS. FINE FOR NON-NOTIFICATION.

A case of unusual interest was heard at the Borough, Court yesterdny, before the Mayor (presiding), and other magistrates, when Hefty Salt, 27, Priestley-street mas charged with failing to give notice of the death of n child which she had adopted, within the time specified by the act, to the Guardians of the Warrington UOlOO. Mr. Shearer stated that this was the first case they bad dealt with under the Coildren's Act of 1903, which enacted that anyone adopting a child under the age of seven years must give notice within 48 biotin of the death of the child. This child died on May 10th and no notice was received by the Union. A mmsiderable time after the child's death an officer viaited the house in the usual way and found that the child was dead. It's death had been registered in the name of Hall whereas its name was Smith. The Guardians did not ask for a severe penalty but the case had been brought before the magistrates as an unusual one, to show that the Act must be obeyed. The child was born on February 19th this year. An officer of the Guardians deposed to the defendant adopting the child on March 9th and to Ws visits on March 14th and 24th, when be made reports that the defendant was a satisfactory person to have charge of the child. On August Bth he visited the house at 44, Garibaldi-street and found that the defendant had removed to 27, Priestley-street. Ile then went to that address and was informed that the child had died. He inquired of the defendant when death took olace, but she said that she could not tell him the date or the month but that it was sometime about Walking Day. The officer informed the defendant that she had com- mitted a very grievious offence and that he would have to report the matter. In reply the defendant said " Von can do what you like." The officer paid a visit to the undertaker and was informed that the child died on May 18th. Mr. Shearer fistated that at the time of the adoption the defendant was given a notice in which it was stated that removal and death had to be notified. _ . . . .

Defendant stated that Dr. Adams bad attended the child from birth and that he gave the death certificate to the child's mother and not to her. She had not time to read the notice as one of the children tore it to pieces before she could see it. Mr. Shearer : The facts are posted on the walls in the town. . . .

The chairman said that the maximum penalty for the breaking of that Act was a very heavy one, the defendant being liable to a flue of £25 or six months' imprisonment. As that was the defendant's first offence and asitbe Guardians did not wish to press the case the Bench bad decided to impose a fine of El, including costs, with the alternative of 14 days' imprisonment.

COUNTY REVISION COURTS.

"LODGER FRANCHISE A MASS OF CONTRADICTION."

Mr. C. H. M. Wharton, revising barrister, sat at St. Helene on Tuesday to deal with the revision of the voters' lists for the St. Helens portion of the Newton Division. Mr. F. J. Downes appeared for the Liberal party, Mr. W. Forshaw the Labour party, and Mr. Arthur Smith and Mr. Mawdaley for the Conservatives. I Mr. George Cross and Mr. W. C. Baker presented the ' lists for the Prescot district, Mr. B. Smith for Rain. ford, Mr. E. Range for Eccleaton, and Mr. H. Twiat for St. Helens. The barrister first dealt with the lodger claims for the d:fferent districts, and as none of the British citizen had consented to attend and subject themselves to cross-examination they were all &truck off with about two exceptions. In the case of Lawrence Kershaw, who claimed a vote for his lodgings in Roskin drive, Mr. Smith said that it was an ordinary lodger claim, there being no relationship between the parties, and the amount paid for the rooms was the full market value. The Barrister said that clod not matter. The lodger franchise was a mass of contradiction. What the lodger paid a week had nothing at all to do with the matter, bot still he bad to put it in his declaration. Ile thought it was intended to show the revising barristkr that the lodger did pay something and that there was a contract; betas to its loll:lancing him in coming to a conclusion on the question of value be did not think it was worth anything. Mr. Smith : But he pays it to a stranger The Revising Bart later : I can only answer as I used Ito do when I first started revising voting lists, that a I man might pay 20s. a week to occupy two rooms that were worth 3s. if his landlady was very good-looking. I (Laughter.) The claim was not allowed. I When the lists had in-en " purged "of lodgers the Barrister generously offered to hear any of them if they appeared to support their claim before the court rose. PENKEIIi TANNERY MANAGER'S CLAIM.

If the occupation is not such as to give him a vote, it is no occupation at all for the purposes of the Act. For instance, you don't suggest that being a shareholder in a limited company would give a maa the right to vote in respect of his occiTatlon ? . .

Mr:Bownes : No, orf oourse not. He has no right to voteas occupier. The Barrister : Then how can you say that Mr Ry.lands occupies? . .

The Barrister: I know, but you must, in order to Idisentitle him to his vote for the county, prove that he is in occupation of the land. I Mr. Downes : He is in joint occupation. I The Barrister : No, he is not in occupation. It could not be put better than by Mr. Coward in a case I decided this year. He sap' . The object of the 24th I Section of the Representation of the People Act, 1832, is to exclude freehold estate occupied by the owner within the borough, NV hieb, by reason of this occupa- tion, may confer the borough franchise, from also con- ferring a vote for the county. Cheshire Courts. GRAPPENH ALL.

On Saturday morning Mr. Clement Elphinstone Llosd, revising barrister, attended in the Large Room at Grappenhall, to revise the lista of voters for the several parishes in the Grappenball polling district, which comprises Grappenhall and Latchford Without. The Liberals were represented by Mr. Cliffe and the Conservatives by Mr. Duckworth. The assistant overseers present were Mr. Joseph Smith for Latchford Without and Mr. C. H. Hoolt for Grappenhall. Tbe Rector (Rev. H. V. Pigot) also attended. Most of the business was of a formal character. UNSUCCESSFUL LODGER CLAIMS.

A number of lodger claims put in on behalf of Liberals gave rise to considerable discussion and most of them were disallowed. It was alleged that in some cases the claimants bad not had exclusive occupation of the rooms in respect of which votes were claimed and one young man was proved to have slept in a room which was als s used as a bath room. It was not, be said, used by any member of the family except swims he Naas on bis holidays. lie did not give any instructions that it was not to ba used during his absence. The Barrister said that if similar claims were made again he should demand the presence of thy claimants and, moreover, be shouid send the declarations which supported the claims to the Public l'rosecutor. lie would also exercise Ms power of inflicting fines upon the claimants.

The Liberal Agent said he endorsed what the Revis- ing Barrister had said. He rvas ignorant of the claims, which had not come through him. The Barrister said the claims were positively fraudulent. We are informed that out of a total of 14 new claims, 8 were disqualified on account of their having removed from one" lodging 'to another or in conse- quence of their falling short—in some cases by weeks only—of the statuory 12 months. Four were under age There is, however, nothing on the declaration as to the age of the claimant.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

OUR "PHIL."

All hail to the Warrington Philomathic Society; likewise more power to its elbow. In view of its extraordinary achievements, it is not stall extravagant to claim that if this enterprising institution had not come into existence the intellectual life of the district would have been decidedly lower than it is. That, indeed, will be readily and gratefully conceded by many, and one need not trouble to support it by the mass of facts and figures ready to hand. Sufficient for the day is the remark that the organisation to which last season we were indebted for the lectures by Shackleton, Chesterton, and Dickens has now ensured a visit by a yet more attractive personality. I rcfer, of course to MISS ELLEN TERRY,

Who so long ago as Jima 19013, had been fifty years on the stage, and still continues to excercise an irresistible fascination over all. As an old colleague of mine—whom in circumstances hinted at hereafter I sought out on Saturday last—observes, the secret of the spell which Miss Terry has cast over playgoers, not less in the American continent than in her native England, is to be found not only in her marvellous gifts as an artiste, but also in her woroanly sympathy and charm—qualities which have endeared her to the public as much as to her private friends. Her artistic genius ranges over a wide field. If it be thought by any that in tragic intenaity she falls somewhat belpw the greatest of her predecessors she has certainly not been excelled in poetic grace and charm, or in tender pathos. There can be no doubt that on November 9th, when the great actress is to give a dramatic recital here, the Parr Hall will be excessively crowded, there- fore seats should be secured in good time. A NOTEWORTHY INCIDENT.

I happen to be fresh from reading Miss Terry's autobiography, in which she has many reminiscences of Sir Henry Irving leading up to the story of her quite friendly sepatation from him, when he took " Faust" and Tennyson's " Beckett " to America and she declined hie invitation to appear therein. I may Jetta° to the subject in a future contribution; here and now I may just repeat the story of how Miss Terry once retired from the stage and bow she was induced to return to it. She writes :—" I left the stage for six years, without the slightest idea of ever going back. I left it without regret. A-id I was very happy, leading a quiet, domestic life in the heart of the country. When my two children were born, I thought of the stage less than ever. They absorbed all my time, all my interest, all my love ! One day, I was driving in a narrow lane, when the wheel of the pony cart came off. I was standing there thinking what I should do next, when a whole crotvd of horsemen in ' pink' came leaping over the hedge into the lane. One of them stopped, and asked me if he could do anything. Then he looked hard at me, and exclaimed, 'Good God, it's Nally ! ' The man was Charles Reade. • Where have you been all these yearn? 'he said. I have been having a very happy time,' I answered. 'Well, you've had it long enough. COlll3 back to the stage ! " No. never ! ' ' You're a fool. You ought to come back.' Suddenly I remembered the bailiff in the house a few miles aw.iy, and I said laughingly. 'Well, perhaps I would think of it if someone would give me forty pounds a week !" Done !' said Charles Reade. 'lll' give you that and more, if you'll come and play Phillippa Chester in 'The Wandering Heir." The offer was accepted. MORRIS DANCING.

• I have intimated that I had another little 'jaunt on Saturday. A Rochdale writer for the Press invited , me to accompany him on a pilgrimage to the quaint old Ashworth Chapel. It stands on a considerable, eminence overlooking Heywoodl(or "Monkey Town,' as irreverent outsiders will persist in calling it), and the burial ground contains an unusually large propor- tion of cunous epitaphs. It is not with these, bow- ever, that lam at present concerned. On the return journey to Rochdale, we had the good fortune to encounter a numerous body of Minns dancers First in popular estimation in mediirval times, the Morris dance has fallen on evil days, and it was refreshing to find that in the Rochdale district this picturesque and ancient form of amusement along with rushbeariug, by-the-bye), still sur- vives, and that apparently in vigour. Professor Skeet Bays it took its name from the Moors. According to what I have read of it, there was a period in our island story when it was reckoned among the foremost of English sports ; most emphatically, the May-day celebration of a by-gone age would have bees considered seriously incomplete without it. As I saw it reproduced on the recent occasion now in question, there was nothing about it that was not perfectly innocent, and one cannot under- ,tand why so innocuous a pastime should have been banned by the good Puritans of two centuries and a half agl. I read that in 1530 complaint was made by one Mr. Edmond Assheton of the "lewd sports of the May Day celebrations of his time, but whether Morris dancing (Morrice is the olden form of spelling) was included among these does not appear, as the complaint does not particularise. As to its origin, I have cited a learned professor who has the suppoit of various autholities ; but I ought to note thatat least one has ventured to differ from these. Strutt, in '• Sports and Pastimss," writes thus—" I make no doubt the Morris dance, which afterwards became exceedingly popular in this country, originated with the fools' dance, and thence we trace the bells which characterized the Morris dancers. The word Morris applied t 3 the dance is usually derived from ' Morisco which in the Spanish language signifies a Moor, as if the dance had been fates from the M ors ; but I , cannot help considering this is a mistake. He goes to state that the Moorish dance was different, there being only one performer, and castanets or rattles being used in it instead of bells. THE COOKERY CENTRE.

" I want to learn to make a jelly," aaid the newly installed housewife. " Is it hard ? " " Oh, Lord,

no, mum," replied the cook, with supreme pity. "It's soft." In that case it was the servant who misunder- sto-d. Take an example in which, so to put it, the bcot was on the other leg. " I want to complain of the flour you sent me the other day," said Mrs. Yonngwife severely. " What was the matter with it, ma'am? " asked the grocer. It was tough. My husband limply wouldn't eat the biscuits I made with it." Judging by the offic'al returns respecting the cookery centre here, Warrington will soon have no further use for the joker whose stock.itetrade consists mainly of such criticisms as the foregoing I rather surprised to learn from one well- I informed woman writer that the s udy of domestic science in the public schools by no means meets with favour everywhere among the parents. Some are indifferent and others sneer and sniff at it contemptu. finely. One affected, opinionated woman, when she first heard that cooking was being taught, could not emces'. her amusement. To her and others like her domestic science meant nothing but dabbling with cooking-lessons—learning culinary capers. Teaching it in the schools was to her a ridiculous absurdity. " I shouldn't want Margaret to waste her time on cooking at school," she remarked " I can teach her that at h me." My own view is that there is plenty of room for extension and develooment of the work so favourably reported upon last week. lam decidedly disposed to agree with the assertion that, taking this country as a whole, we are a long way behind in the matter of domestic management and economy. Should any shade of doubt be cast upon this statement, let any who have had long experience in the management of an establishment of their own look back upon the years that are sped, upon the ignorance and con- sequent failures that have obtained through them, and were only with much difficulty and much loss finally overcome —mistakes that could for the most port have been avoided by a systematic training in early life. Assuredly such expenditure and waste need never have been if the leading lines of domestic managament and economy had been well drilled into the mind definitely and precisely by efficient teachers from earliest childhood as is now more than ever the case in Warrington. Moreover, as I have stated, there is ample room for further improvement along the lines indicated. A while ago I dipped into a timely little handbook for elementary teachers. It is entitled " Home Management: A Three Years' Course for Schools," by Miss Wilena Hitching, headmistress of Meanwood-road Girls' School, Leeds, published by Messrs. W. and R. Chambers Miss Hitching proposes a course of lessons, very simple and homely in character, all designed to teach girls how to manage homes in a wholesome, healthy, and thrifty manner. These lessons she has been giving is her own school for some time past, with much accep'acce by her pupils and their parents. There are lessons on personal cleanliness, on domestic work, on the punch no and preparation of good food on the choice of dress, on the choice of wallpapers and furniture, and on the care of infants. Surely girls should first and above all else be fitted for the duties of life they will have to perform when they have homes of their own. One would have supposed it so obvious as to go without saying that girls who are destined to become helpmeets for shopkeepers, artisans, clerks, miners, mill operatives and the like need not have too much time spent upon them in the relation to the art of mixing colours and painting flowers, in the principles of citizenship, and other sub- jects foreign to the work of keeping a home together economically and at the same time at its highest efficiency. SWIMMING.

" Must it really be kept a secret that the dogma of physical exercise is a fabric of imagination? "asks Professor Hugo Munsterberg, the Harvard psychologist in his recent book, " Problems of Today" and he goes on—" If once we liberate ourselves from the hygienic cant with which our time is overflooded we must recognise the comic aspect of the situation. Millions of people are running wildly to catch a ball, lifting weights in fullest perspiration, trotting with gasping breath, and doing a hundred other useless , stunts simply because a meaningless fashion has cruelly thrown them into such a habit." There's a great deal more to the like effect, but the point I wish for the moment to emphasise is that the professor's most extraordinary denunciation of various forms of athleticism leaves unaffected the noble art of which the local Swimming Club gave so striking a demonstration on Thursday evening. Every boy and as many gills as possible should lest n to swim, and the more proficient the rising race becomes in this the better. Even those who lift up their voices against football and raid against other p,pular spouts and pastimes must admit and even commend this. WEATHER TO ORDER.

Last week's note on the local rainpour brought to my mind something said by Mr. S. Z de Ferranti in the course of his presidential address to the Institute of Electrical Engineers. He solemnly avowed his belief that the time would come when it would be thought no more wonderful largely to control our weather than it was n tar thought wonderful to control the water after it had fallen on the land. He thought that it would be possible to acquire knowledge which would enable us largely to control by electrical means the sunshine which reached us, and in a climate which usually had ample moisture in the atmosphere to pro- duce rainfall when and where we required it It might be possible when we knew a great deal more about electricity than we did to-day, to net up an electrical defence along our coasts by which we could cause the moisture in the clouds to fall in the form of rain, and so prevent the-e clouds (Lifting over the country between ourselves and the sun, instead of so ' often blotting the latter out—though I fancy few readers of he"t Examiner" will this season have any complaint to make o that sco-e. To conclude, how- ever, the speaker referral to said it also seemed to him that it would he putsible, when more water on the country was required, to cause the falling of rain from the clouds passing over the highest part of the country, and so produce an abundance of water which properly used, would greatly add to the fertility of the country. That is all right. So mate it be—and 0 let it be soon. Still, I for one must confess that I " has ma doots."

The C.L.B. and the Cadet Scheme.

There is at the present time a great deal of discussion among the governing bodies of the various lads' organizations in the country over the question of recognition by the Territorial Associations. The Church 'Ads' Brigade is aft ected by the turmoil. The governing body of this organization declare that they i did not seek recognition, but are prepared to accept t now they have been assured that the spiritual side of their work will not be impsired by the working of this Cadet schnoe. The Lord Chief Justice of England (Lord Alverstone), speaking on the subject of recogni- tion at the last annual meeting of the C.L.B. said : "Having studied the question with the greateal care, I do honestly believe that by connection with the Territorial Association the tyisiti 30 of the Church Lads' Brigade will be strengthened and its power tor good will increase." A compiny at Knotaford has already been granted recognition and its officers have received their commissions.

Wycliffe P S.A. Society. There was a very large gathering at the open meeting of this society on Sanday. Mr. E. Osborne presided and said how pleased he was to preside over that large meeting. The various parts of the world were now so closely connected that a visit could be paid t 3 several countries in a very short time, whereas fifty years ago it took • very lengthened period. Men would say even now that the old days were better than these. That he did not believe. There were more Christian people to-day than ever. More work was being done for Christ than ever. The Gospel was being preached in every part of the world. Voltaire said, more than on hundred years ago, that in one hundred years Christianity would be played out. Two thousand years ago Christ said "I, if I be lifted up, shall draw all moo unto Ha." Tasty must not be in a hurry. Scientists said the world as we knew it took millions of years to c•eate. On the Welland, at Kirkstead, Lincs., the annual fresh-water angling contest has lust been held. LADIES' BILLIARD MATCH.

Oae by one the quieter branches of sport are being invaded by women. Miss Ruby Roberts this week acquired the distinction of being the first of her sex to win a public billiard match. Miss Roberts's opponent was Miss E. Collins, and in a game of 400 up, ker best break was one of 50. The match was held in the Minor Hall, Leicester Square, London. Miss Roberts plays an exceedingly graceful game, and the match was followed with close merest. She will no doubt have to meet a good many challenges if shc decides to call herself Lady Billiard Champion, as she is being urged to do.

M. STOLYPIN.

The wounds inflicted upon M. Stolypin. the Russian Premier, by an assassin during a gala performnnce at the Opera at Kieff, proved fatal on Monday evening last. His recovery at one time seemed assured. It is understood that the assassin was also a Police spy, and that his duplicity had been discovered. It was to save himself from death that he undertook the murder of Ma Ihemier.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, UP. sliouLD THE NATION OWN THE RAILWAYS?

The labour trouble in the railway service is going to compel the country to consider the question of railway nationalisation. The Independent Labour Party is about to start a national campaign in favour of that step.' But fortunately this is not a question which , appeals to Socialists only; it is not among those who have accepted the Collectivist ideal only that support will be found for the proposal that the State should own and work the railways. There is a great deal of opinion favourable to this suggestion among all political parties and among the com- mercial classes. The Trades Union Congress has repeatedly declared in favour of State railways ; so I believe has the Co-operative Congress. The Commercial Travellers Association is strongly in favour ; and great commercial men like Sir John Brunner are advocates of nationally owned and nationally worked railways. Quite recently a Com- mission reported in favour of the State acquiring the Irish Railways, and this is the more remarkable when it is remembered that the Chairman of this Commission, was Chairman of one of the largest English rail- ways. At least two of the most influential members of the present government are known to be strongly in favour of the State purchase of the railways.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
SPLENDID ISOLATION.

In 1844 Sir Robert Peel's Government passed an Act empowering the State to pur- chase the railways'made since that time by giving three months' notice of their intention to do so. The terms on which the purchase was to be made are laid down in the Act. At the present time Great Britain is the only country in Europe, almost the only country in the world, where the railways are wholly in private hands. The United States stands with Great Britain in this position of cold isolation. In recent years several countries have nationalised their railways in whole or part. Among these countries are Switzerland, Italy, Turkey, France and Japan. A BUSINESS PROPOSITION.

This then is a question, which can be discussed as a business proposition. It is not enough to condemn the proposal because Socialists advozate it. It may be Socialism; but at any rate it is a system which, as has been said, practically every other country has adopted. But whether it is Socialism or not does not affect the merits of the question. If it would be a good thing to have the State take over the railways, he would be far removed from a wise man who objected to the proposal because somebody said it was Socialism. The National Telephone system is to pass into the hands of the Government at the end of this year, and it might be remembered that it was a Unionist Govern- ment who made the contract of purchase, and a Liberal Government who completed the transfer.

Fortunately the British people usually take a common sense view of any question they can be got to seriously consider. That is the reason why there is so much I practical Socialism in this country, and so few adherents of the Socialist parties. The Britisher is not much of a theorist. If he can see a practical advantage in a particular proposal he is not going to refuse to take that advantage because somebody gives the thing a nickname. The question of railway nationalisation must be considered, apart from theories, from three points of view ; (1) the benefit to the community ; (2) the benefit to trade and commerce ; (3) the benefit to the railwaymen. THE FINANCIAL ASPECT.

In one column of a newsaper one cannot deal fully with all or with any one of these points. Not more than a very briefly summarised case can be put forward. The community will gain because it will eventu- ally save the profits which now go to private shareholders. Last year the British railways received £123,925, 565 for goods and freight. Out of that sum they paid £47,355,889 to their shareholders. That is to say, that out of every shilling the railway companies received, over 4d. went into the pockets of the shareholders as dividend. In 1909, the last year for which I have returns, the German State railways paid as profit into the Imperial Exchequer sum of 123,000,000 marks. This was done after giving rates which are not more than half the rates on the British railways. The fact that one nation after another has taken over privately managed railways is in itself proof that the experience of State Railways commends the method.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COMMUNITY INTEREST.

The community would gain a great advan- tage from having the railways managed by a public department which was under public control. The Board of Trade has a certain amount of control over the railways, but it is really very little. The Board cannot regulate hours or wages, and the Railway Commissioners can only deal with rates within certain limits. If we had State Railways there would be a Minister in the House of Commons responsible for every- thing connected with the railways; just as the Postmaster General is there to answer for the work of the Post Office.

The traders of the country would gain by a better service. That is so certain that it does not need to be argued. If one has any doubts upon the point let him call to mind the telegraph system when it was in private hands; or let him contrast the present public street railway systems in Glasgow, Leeds, Liverpool, or anywhere else with the privately owned systems they superseded. If one wants a contrast of rates let him com- pare the rates for goods and passengers on the German or Belgian State railways with the rates on British railways. Railway com- fort is largely a matter of national standard. In some countries where the general standard of comfo:t is low, the standard on the State rai.ways, is not so high as on the British railways, but in countries like Germany the accommodation is much superior for a lower charge. I have no space to give comparisons of rates, but anyone who wishes to pursue the subject will find abundant matter of that sort in any work dealing with State Railways.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOWING THE WAY.

The British railways have themselves shown by their policy in recent years that there is a good deal of avoidable waste in the working of the railways. They have come to working agreements to save the multiplication of services. A great deal of the capital which has been put into British railways has been required to enable one company to compete for the traffic which some company was already handling. The trader may think that it is an advantage to him to have the choice of two or three rail- ways. But he is really under a delusion if he thinks that. One railway may offer him

better facilities than another, but he must remember that all the competing railways have enormous expenses through the com- petition, and these have to be paid by the traders.

.•••, ,w 1

===== 0002601_19110923_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
AT THE PUBLIC EXPENSE.

The Board of Trade have promised the railway companies that they shall be allowed to increase their legal rates if it should be necessary to ask them to improve the con- ditions of the workmen. That is, the trader has to pay. This would not happen under public ownership. The Post Office is regularly improving the wages of the staff, but at the same time it is giving 'more and cheaper facilities to the public. Can anybody imagine a Postmaster General raising the price of a penny stamp in order to increase the wages of the staff? Any well-managed business is always effecting economies, and in a State owned monopoly these savings would go to improve the service or to better labour conditions. As I have mentioned before, the British Consul at Frankfort says that the low rates given to traders by the German State Railways have done more than any other aid to build up Germany's great commercial position. FOR THE WORKERS' BENEFIT.

The railway servants would undoubtedly benefit from State ownership. The wages of railwaymen were lower in 1910 than in 1907. In the meantime the Post Office, a Govern- ment institution, had raised wages by about three-quarters of a million a year. The railwaymen would probably not be allowed to strike if the railways were under the Government, and that, I think, is a regula- tion which can be defended. But the rail- waymen would have votes then, and that would alter the whole situation in regard to striking. The Government is pledged to be a model employer, and it could not claim to be that if it kept the wages of over 100,000 railwaymen at less than a pound a week.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE NEAR FUTURE.

This question of railway nationalisation is coming to the front. Men who bad never thought seriously about the subject have been induced to do so by recent events. Outside the ranks of the Socialists there are many men who think that an essential public service which is of the nature of a monopoly should be owned and managed by the com- munity. It is rather an extraordinary thing that Great Britain, who is so far advanced in the direction of public ownership, should be about the last to take over the railways. It is clear from the Act of 1844 that State purchase was seriously contemplated then as a probability. It is now one of the assured things of the not very distant future, and the British people, with their extra- ordinary genius for self-government and public administration will make a success of State owned railways.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
Pe•Nrs..A.,--"."^"....\,,--",,,,,‘"Ad iCHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB. N,—,Nowvevws.r.,N.-1/4,-^v-v

GEORGE WASHINGTON'S COURTESY. It is always a joy to meet a real gentle- man or lady, and the next best thing to meeting with them is to read about them. I came across a passage the other day which occurs in a book written about George Washington, the famous President of the United States and it gives such a fine picture of his thoughtfulness that I will print it here for all my nieces and nephews to read. The lady who wrote the book was Martha L. Phillips, a granddaughter of General Nathaniel Green, who used to visit Wash- ington and who saw much of his private life. This is the story : " One incident which occurred during that visit was so comical in itself, and so characteristic of Washington, that I recall it for your entertainment. Early in a bright winter morning a droll-looking old countryman called to see the President. In the midst of their interview breakfast was announced, and the President invited the visitor, as was his hospitable wont on such occasions, to a seat beside him at the table. The visitor drank his coffee from his saucer ; but, lest any grief should come to the snowy damask, he laboriously scraped the bottom of his cup on the saucer's edge before setting it down on the tablecloth. He did it with such audible vigor that it attracted my attention and that of several young people present, always on the alert for occasions of laughter. We were so indiscreet as to allow our amusement to become obvious. General Washington took in the situation, and immediately adopted his visitor's method of drinking his coffee, making the scrape even more pronounced than the one he reproduced. Our disposition to laugh was quenched at once."

YOU WILL NEVER BE SORRY For living a pure life. For doing your level best. For being kind tl the poor. For looking before leaping. For bearing before judging. For thinking before speaking. For harboring clean thoughts. For standing by your principles. For stopping your ears to gossip. For asking p3rdon when in error. For being generous ter an enemy. For being square in business dealings. For giving an unfortunate person a lift. For promptness in keeping your promises. For putting the beet construction on the alt others.

HALF-AN-HOUR IN GRENDON UNDERWOOD.

WHERE SHAKESPEARE DREAMED HIS "MID-SUMMER NIGHT'S DREAM."

It was by woeful mismanagement that we only had half-an-hour, but in a strange land is is easy to misjudge time and distance, and if we failed to do justice to Grendon Under- wood, we stumbled upon another spot which the untravelled reader may be glad to hear about.

But why race from Oxford and tramp over several miles to see a half-lost village in Oxfordshire ? For no reason at all save that it boasts a Shakespeare Association and tells a possibly-true yarn of our very greatest poet,

• There are two Underwoods in Oxfordshire. Wooton Underwood at which we found our- selves, and Grendon Underwood to which we tramped. 13oth were originally written Bernwode, a name which describes a position in the low-lying meadows around Bernwood Forest. Grendon comes from Greendon, or hill, and was formerly Greendon-under- Bernwood, and the Oxford uncomplimentary distich says Grendon Underwood Dirtiest place that ever stood That, however, referred to the dirty torn- up road along which the coaches and pack- horses travelled in the long ago. It was because the main road led this way

Those who want to see the two constables will find them in the play. They are there as Dogberry and Verges in "Much ado about Nothing.

wa-s a summer night when he took up his lodging in the porch and the mists of the early dawn brought fairies and songs. And thus arose the famous "Midsummer Night's Dream."

There is really no doubting these things when you are told them on the spot. We went to see the chnrch but could find no porch. There is, however, a drawing to be seen made by order of tho Lord of the Manor previous to its demolition.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
RELICS.

The old beams of the inn are black with age, and the old oak staircase with quaint banisters and posts is unaltered since the spacious days of Elizabeth. An old table remains and a weather vane, and there is also a curious candlestick made from mulberry wood. Under a mulberry tree, formerly in the garden, and now shaped into relies such as this Shakespeare sat to weave his plots. It is to the historian Aubrey, who lived some twenty years after the poet, that we are indebted for the story of Much ado about nothing," and Sheehan tells the story' in greater detail. That he stayed here is ' highly probable and a tradition that dates to his own generation can hardly be dismissed. He would pass it on the way from Stratford to London and may have formed friendships which held him here. _

The known facts concerning Shakespeare form a scrap heap which no historian has yet joined together. This scrap, thought it be but a scrap, is piquant enough to be refreshing.

THE OLDE SHIPPE INNE,

We seek the old place that once echoed to the coachman's horn and find it half way down the village. It is now a placid farm known as " Shakespeare's House," but was formerly the coaching Inn and its old sign, on which Shakespeare gazed, is among the treasures of the Bucks Archeological Society. With the bright summer sun shining through luxuriant elms and with high old hedgrows to shelter us we are in the mood for believing every happy thing that is told us, and it takes no effort to persuade us that Shakespeare stopped there often, that he delighted to stop there, that he stopped for inspiration and got it Why shouldn't he? The place is good enough. The old house is a good bit of sixteenth century architecture and would be in the glory of its youth when the poet saw it. It has three stories and served until about a , century ago as a wayside inn. The gabled I end is the portion to which they lead U 8 to show the veritable chamber in which he wrote. Upon the third floor is a curious oval window which lights the chamber in which he is said to have slept, and upon the ground floor is a large guest room with an uncqmmon stone fire-place and a latticed window. It is rather awkward to contradict a flat statement that Shakespeare wrote two plays there (though giving the lady sixpence ought to confer rights of some kind) and there is nothing for it but to listen to the story. HOW SHAKESPEARE GOT HIS INSPIRATION.

Arriving one afternoon (so the local legend runs) and finding his money unequal to the strain of a night at the inn, the poet decided to rest in the porch of the church and there fell asleep. The two village constables were told that a suspicious character had been seen and made bold to accuse him of wanting to rob the sanctury. They were proceeding to use him roughly when Shakespeare asked to be allowed to see the interior. He gave a keen glance around on entering and fAiling to see anything of value grimly remgrked that they were making much ado about nothing."

CHAPTER XXI.

'DRIVE TO SCOTLAND YARD."

When Lady Jane drove away, Rebecca could scarceiy contain herself. Strong un- easiness possessed her. She could not rest. She felt almost sorry at having allowed her old friend to go out alone. Why had she been so cowardly as not to accompany her? This was the first time in her own life that Re- becca had accused herself of the sin of cowardice. She was, as • rule, the bravest of the brave; and she hated herself now as she had never done before for putting her poor friend, as she feared, into an uncomfortable . position . . . _ . . .

c It is true Lady Jane had gone away having faithfully promised Rebecca not to visit the Mina; but Rebecca had considerable power of reading character. A certain intuition in her misnd told her that Lady Jane was full of curiosity, and also full of ignorance. She did not know the true position. She was, there- fore, quite unprepared for the dangers which might await her. The subtlety and cleverness of the Mirza were quite beyond her power to grasp. Rebecca could not keep still. At one time she was so distressed about her old friend that she even thought of going to the Carlton to inquire if. by any chance, she was there. On second thoughts, however, she did not do that; although her fear and distress took such possession of her that for the time she almost forgot Lonsdale and his grave danger. The Mina Kara Yoga was always quick in his movements, and although Lady Jane felt the minutes turning into hours while she re- mained a prisoner at the Carlton Hotel, yet in reality it was but • little after four o'clock when the carriage returned and Lads Jane's

fetter was put into ncoecca's 'hands. The girl read it once; she read it twice: "Lady Jane Halliday remains as a guest of the Mirza Kara Yusuf until Rebecca Hen- shall calls to see her." The immaculate footman, George, had brought the letter to Rebecca, who was in the drawing-room. She looked up at him at once. "Please tell the coachman that I want the carriage immediately," said Rebecca. "This letter is from your mistress, George. She has given me a message which must be attended to at once." Rebecca flew to her room. Silver was in her mistress's bedroom. "Silver," said the girl, "will you come with me!" "Where to, and why!" asked Silver in some astonishment. "It is on account of your mistress, and I can explain nothing, but I think there ought to be two of us. Come at once." has anything happened to Lady Jane?" asked Silver, ill a voice of aatoniahment. "I hope not," replied Rebecca, "but she has asked me to go to her." "Where is she, ma'am?" "She is at the Carlton Hotel. She went to see the Persian gentleman who cared this .morning." "Oh, the gentleman with a queer name!" said Silver. "My missis always did lose foreigners; and she wants you, miss--" "To go to her to the Cal:lton. I have her letter. Come, Silver, be quick." The woman hurried to dress herself. She was devoted to Lady Jane. She could not understand, however, why Miss Henshall looked RO white and uneasy. As Rebecca was leasing the house, her maid from Paddock Row, came up the steps. "I've brought you this, miss," she said, and she put a telegram into Rebecca's hand The girl opened it. Her eyos flashed with pleasure. It contained hut the one word "Safe," and bore the postmark of a little vil- lage towards the north of England. "Thank you, Lucy; that is all right," said Rebecca. . .

She ran into the dining-room, thrust the telegram into the fire, and then, relieved to a certain extent, entered the carriage. "I want you," she said to the footman, who stood by waiting for directions, "to drive at once to Scotland-yard." Not a muscle of the man's face changed. He gave the order to the coachman and mounted on the box beside him. Silver, how- ever, was by no means so undemonstrative. "I thought we were going to the Carlton Hotel," she said. "So we are presently," said Rebecca, "but I wish to go to Scotland-yard first." "I don't understand you, miss." "I am sorry, Silver; I am doing the best I can according to my lights." "Your manner, miss, seems to imply that my—my mistress is somehow, in some sort of danger." , "Oh, I hope not," answered Rebecca; "but ' we'll ensure her safety by going to Scotland' yard first." "But miss," said Silver again, frighten me, miss." Rebecca was silent, but just for one minute she laid her hand on the woman's arm. The pressure was at once affectionate and confiding. Silver looked at the young girl, as though on the spot she was beginning to love her. "She isn't like most," thought the lady's maid. She has a heart as well as a head. My poor, dear, sweet mistress is all heart. Often and often do we ssrvants wish that she wasn't so impulsive. Now this young lady would go through any danger for one she loved, but she wouldn't be silly over it." They arrived at Scotland-yard. Rebecca left the carriage, desired Silver to keep her seat, and went in. She saw the Chief Super- intendent of Police, and frankly and simply put a few particulars of the case before him. She said nothing whatever about Lonsdale, for she felt that silence with regard to him was her best policy, but she told the Super- intendent that the Mirza missed a valua'ule diamond, that through a chain of unforseen circumstances, she, Rebecca Henshall, was mixed up in the loss, that, in short, a trick had been played upon her. She had been rendered unconscious, and the diamond which her father held in trust had been ex- changed for a false stone. The Mirza bad just discovered the trick which bad been played upon Rebecca and upon himself. He had written twice to her that day to ask her to call to see him. She had taken no notice of his invitation. Ile had then made a per- sonal call on Lady Jane Halliday, during which visit he used his utmost influence to induce the lady to bring Rebecca with her to tea with him at the Carlton. Rebecca had refused to go, and Lady Jane, yielding to her entreaties, had written a line to the Mimi', changing her mind about accepting his •iiivi- tation. Now, matters were changed. She had gone out to pay sonic' calls, intending to leave a note of refusal at the Carlton. She had not returned, and Rebecca was in pos- session of the following message. Here the girl put Laity Jane's short missive into the Superintendent's hand. "And do you mean to go?"' he asked, look- ing full at her. "Certainly," was the reply, "how can I possibly do otherwise?" "I should recommend you," said the Superintendent, "not to go to the Carlton Hotel. Lady Jane Halliday is, doubtless, quite safe, and when she finds you don't obey her will return home. You might send her a note by messenger, however, to say you cannot go. If she is unlawfully detained after that then we must interfere ;_ but nothing has occurred so far which could cause Scotland- yard to interfere in the matter. Then she added, "The fact is I am only telling you a part of the truth." "Then can you possibly expect me to act?" was the response. "I cannot tell you the rest, for it impli. rates another." "Indeed?" "Yes," said Rebecca, "and to the point of extreme danger—danger to life. I cannot and will not tell you about this, but I know what I am saying, and I want your assistance if you can give it to me." . said the Superinten- dent," arrest the irOM II who stole the dia- mond, and who attempted your life in so doing." , "There is no time even to think of her," ' said Rebecca. "I have had a letter from I her, and she is out of England." ' "Ah! then I don't see what is to be done. , The Orientals are always troublesome to ' deal with—they are a law unto themselves. We don't want to make ourselves unpleasant to Persia." "Then you won't help me?" said Rebecca,

"I —you

rising. "Have you come here alone?" "I have brought Lady Jane's maid with nie." _

"Ah ! that is good. I will tell you what I can do, if you are determined to call at the Carlton. I will send a very clever and gentlemanly person with you in the carriage. He has had dealings before with foreigners, and is aware of sorne of their methods. What I would suggest, under these circumstances, is this : Drive to the Carlton; ask to be shown at once to the Mirza's apartment. Take the gentleman with you—he will call himself Mr. Saunders. If you can get him admitted to the Mirza's rooms, so much the better; but it is extremely likely that en- trance will be denied him, in which case he will wait for you in the corridor." "Yea," said Rebecca. . .. . . .

"If you do not come out within an hour he will go downstairs and tell your maid, who will immediately return here, when we will send two or three of our men to liberate you, but I do not expect for a single moment that anything serious will happen. This is the best that I can suggest for the time." Here the Superintendent spoke through a telephone. and in a very few minutes a grave, nice-looking, middle-aged man in the ordinary dress of an Englishman entered the room. "A word alone with you, Mr. Saunders," said the Superintendent. Ile took the man into another room. They returntql in • minute or two. Saunders bowed to Rebecca. "I am at your service, madam,'• be said. Rebecca an Saunders entered Lady Jane's carriage. "To the Carlton HoteL" said Rebecca,

ahei tiwy or6ce ilatat

Poor Silver was , looking cold and frightened, and the appearance of Saunders mystified her so much that she scarcely knew herself. _ . .

..,.... "I have ell directions," said Saunders in a low voice, just bending towards Rebecca as they approached the big hotel. "I know exactly what to do. If you are asked any questions about me, you must say that I am your cousin, who has called unexpectedly to see you at Lady Jane Halliday's house." "But that's a lie!" burst from Silver. "It is necessary, madam," said the detec- tive. "I will explain to you later on." "I wish I hadn't come,''' said Silver. "Oh, my poor mistress! what can have happened to her! It's broad daylight, and here are we driving through London to one of the most fashionable hotels in the place, and yet any- one would think we were going to a den of thieves where we'd all be murdered." "You will not say a word of this aloud, will you?" said Saunders. "Yon will keep your sznsations to yourself. You are in a position of trust, and it lies in your power, if the worst happens—which I by no means expoct—to save both your mistress and this young lady." "What am I to do, sir? I am sure I will do anything," said the poor woman. "When we arrive at the Carlton—" "No, sir," interrupted Silver, speaking with great decision. "I won't—l won't go in! " "We don't ask you to," answered Saunders in a gentle voice. 'You have just to remain in the carriage. I trust Lady Jane and Miss Henihall will soon join you, but your part in the present business is to wait in the carriage for further orders." "Heaven preserve us!" murmured Silver. "Now listen," said Saunders. "Not a word to the servants on the box. You are in a position of trust, remember." "Mum I'll be," answered Silver. . .

They reached 'the hotel. Rebecca imme- diately entered, followed by Saunders. He was a good-looking, gentlemanly man, and IN hen Rebecca asked if the Mil za Kara Yusuf, was within, a dark servant imme- diately came forward and, with is low bow, inquired if she were Miss Henshall. Rebecca replied in the affirmative. "Then my master expects you, madam," he said. "May I take you this way?" Rebecca and Saunders were taken to the first floor in the lift. They walked down a spacious corridor and paused before a closed door. Two men in Persian dress were stand- ing like sentinels outside. The man who had accompanied Rebecca and Saunders was in Erglish costume. It was only the darkness of his face, and the peculiar tiute-like quality nt his voice, which -.betrayed his foreign Ile murmured a word to one of the ser- vants. The man instantly opened the door of the Mirza's room, bowed low to that gen- tleman, and gave him a message. He re- turned almost immediately. "The Mirza Kara Timid, will base much pleasure in seeing Miss Henshall. Will the gentleman who has accompanied her remain in another room during the interview." "I should like my friend, Mr. Saunders— my cousin, I mean—to accompany me," said Rebecca." The man bowed. "It is not permissible, madam; you must enter alone." Saunders gave Rebecca a reassuring smile. "I will stay here," he said. "In another room, perhaps, sir?" said the servant. "No, I prefer to remain in the corridor. I do nut suppose you will be long, Miss Hen- shall." "Only a minute or two," said Rebecca, speaking as bravely as she could, "just to see Lady Jane and bring her away with me. She must be tired of waiting." She went bravely forward. The two ser- vants flung the doors wide, at the same time cleverly manipulating the heavy velvet cur- tains. They opened and closed behind He. bona Henshall.

THE HERO OF LABRADOR, Alice Stronach contributes to the " Quiver' the story of the boyhood and youth of Greaten of Labrador. He was born and brought up at Parkgate, a Elblag village on the estuary of the Dee, a few miles from Chester. Young Grenfell was in no way remark- able as child or boy. But both he and his brother loved adventure and an open-air life. They shared the same passion for sport, and delighted in firearms and explo- sives. They spent their days in adventuring on the sands of Dee, tracking tea-tow I, and hailing with fisher- men in their boats. They learned to endure cold and hunger. They set out on a canoe they built for them- selves, camping out overnight to be ready to join the fishing boats at breakfast. They used also to camp out in a little wooden shanty on an island. A peep into a mission tent:at Whitechapel made youag Greaten— then a medical strident—feel that his religious life was a humbug, and he vowed either to make it real or give it up. He made it real. He started as teacher of a clue of young hooligans, and made his room a seek' night gyo madam for them. Sir Fred,rick Treves was asked for a young doctor to take up work for the Mission to Deep Sea Fishermen. He recommended Greaten. So he began hie great career.

Food Prices.—'• Everything to be &wee—. Prices likely to thornier, this winter." Such are the headline., which a Londoo newspaper gives to an article fore- shadowing a mid further general rise of food prices. On the name day, the leading trade journal dealing with agricultural produca, asks whether in many instances the present high prices are justified, seei_g that they do hot accord with market quotations. It points out that whilst flour of every quality has declined in price since a year ago, the average price of bread has risen. Then with regard to the !Anion meat market., there has been a decline in price, in three classes, Os compared with twelve months ago, and no change in other claeses and the gime thing applies to poultry and egge, whilst the wholesale price of Irish bacon is lower. Due can understand that the drought., which has been general over Western Europe, has particularly affected the supply of beetroot, sug ir, and hotter, loot as regards sone provisions, at lease, it seems to be the old story over again, and the excuse of increased cost of distribution owing to strikes and higher wriges, does not justify the extent to which retail prices have been put up, whilst the producer is receiving no incre, or even less, than he did. It is this anomaly that has drawn the French housewives in many towns to go to such extreme lengths as open eoting in their revolt against the continucd increase of food prices. Overolothed —The Medical Officer for Winchester, in the course of his annual report, his given non useful little homily on the clothing of children. Tnere are, of course, among the very poorest, CAMPS of insulticieutly clad children, but Dr. Lyater testifies that "it is far more common to meet children wearirg an excessive quantity of clothing." The report affords confirmation of the opinion to which very many people with experi- ence on the subject have come, that there are not a few parents who are unlit to have the charge of children, and it into be feared that these parents are precirely Use people to whom it is of least use to offer advice. The more sensible mothers must sorely be impressed with the council of the Medical Officer, who thowe by several ludicrous examplen, that there are many children whose lives are being endangered by the extreme solicitude of their parents. There can be no doubt that many more children take cold through being over-clothed rather than under-clothed, aud when they do take cold through over-clothing, the over-elisions parent is inspired with " the idea of adding another thick garment to the already overstocked wardrobe, and the evil is thus intensified." At the seine time Dr. Lyster has found that while the trunk is heavily burdened by unnecessary layers, the limbs are often insufficiently clothed. OVERTON HILL FIRICD.—At Frodadasm Sessions on Wednesday, Dick Ball, of Frodsham Lordship, was charged with setting fire to the bracken and underwood on Overton Hill, on August 13th.—Mr. Linnaker, who appeared to prosecute on behalf of the owner, the ' Marquis of Cholmondeley, said the fire spread very rapidly, and while a number of employees were engaged in endeavouring to put it out, the defendant was seen to set fire to some bracken. The spot was one of the prettiest in Cheshire before the Pre. The damage amounted to hundreds of pstinds.—The defendant said he lighted a cigarette and threw the match down, and he then heard someone shout that he bed net the gorse on fire. He immediately went tack and beat out the fire with a gorse bosh before three pennyworth of damage was done.—ln fining the defendant 21s, inclusive or in default one month, the chairman said the hill was a great asset to tha people of Frodsham and it was their duty to protect it.

••••••••••. ••••••• --- DEATH OF FORMER VICAR OF GREAT SANKF.Y.—It is with regret we record the death of the Rev. Edwin Sandys Jackson, who was for 19 years vicar of St. Mary's Church, Great Sankey. The rev. gentleman, had been living in retirement for the past 11 years at Monkstown, near Cork, and since May of this year be had been in failing health. He pasaedaway early on Sunday morning at the residence of Dr. Orpin (Cork). As vicar of this parish be had a happy and succeeratul time. Ile was much loved by his parishioners for his genial and kindly disposition and his unfailing generosity to all who were in need. During his v icar iate several improvements and additions were made, such as the restoration of St. Mary's Church, the building of St. Mary's Mission Sunday School, Penketh, Sankey Sund ty schools, and a chapel in the Cemetery. He also con- tributed .er..50 to the building fund of the new handsome vicarage in which his many friends hoped he would reside ; tut, owing to continued ill-health, he never occupied the residence, and was ultimately compelled to resign the living. He wis educited at Trinity C4llege, Dublin, where he graduated. His firint curacy was at Sutton, near St. Helens, his second at Farnworth nese Widnes, and finally be was appointed vicar of St. Mary's Church. The funeral took place on Wednesday afternoon at Sankey in the presence of a large and representative gathering. The Rev. J. R. Jones (•icar of Sankey) officiated at the service both in the church and at the cemetery.

PRI M ITI \lc METHODIST ClitillCPl HARVEST FESTIVAL SERVICES.—Tbe holding of special services for the ostensible purpose of giving thanks for the safe garner- ing of the fruits of the fleld,too often fail to synchronise with the ingathering of the final sheaf, and the premature sinf Mg of certain well known hymen consequently incites to a harmless sarcasm. The present autumn is however an exception to the general rule, and, owing to the early season, we can sing with conlldence,even in the middle of September, " All is safely gathered in." On Sunday last two impressive and appropriate sermons I were preached in the above church by Mr. J. W. Willintan of the Manchester College, who deftly handled I his subject, and showed, as in a mirror, how the reflection of God is to be seen is the world of nature. The congregation heartily joined in singing the hackneyed hymns which have done duty on like occasions for so many years, and the choir rendered Simper's anthem " Enter into His gates " with spirit and precision. Surely it is not too much to hope that out of the plethora of writers in the land, sman may be found who will provide us, in the near future, with a few good harvest hymns which we can uselalung with, or to place of, the veterans now waiting their well-earned rest i—On Monday a Service of Song, " The Harvest of the Home " interspersed with anthems, was given by the choir. The various numbers were well sung, and reflected the care and patience which the singers Ind evidently exercised in the preparation of the music. The several solos were effectively rendered by Misses M. .7. Appleton and Dora Cross, and Messrs. H. Clare, J. Wibberley, 'l' Newton, and G W Cross. The narrative was very distinctly and expressively read by the Rev R W B Whitevray, the resident minister. Miss Shore provided the acctmpaniments in her best style, and the conductor was Mr Edward.. The nasal sale was efterwards held in the schoolroom, when both the prices cbtained and the general hilarity of the company reached a high level. The church was very tastefully decorated for the festival, and the officials are deeply indebted both to the friends who provided the seasonable offerings and to those who arranged the gifts with such pleasing effect. The rongregatit.ns were good throughout and the financial result above the average. PETTY SESSIONS.

An interesting though complicated case was heard I when Joseph Lord, insurance agent, of Woodbine-road, I Sandy-lane, Lyman, summoned Henry Thompson, a retired seaman, of the Mariners' Home, Egremont, Cheshire, for feloniously stealing four blankets (valued at 285.) the property of the said Joseph Lord. Prosecutor said that by the will of Emily Florence or Kerrigan who died on 14th May of this year, he was left with her property and insurance money (amounting in all to £2B). He and the decease' were friendly neighbours and he used to go into the house and read to her, as she was blind. In pursuance of ithe will he got it proved at Chester and he heA the probate, which he produced. Thompson, who was the deceased's brother, attended the funeral and witness told him that there was a will in his (witness's) favour, at which the defendant expressed displeasure. ST. HELENS.

NEW BOROUGH SURVEYOR.

Mr. Arthur W. Bradley, who has been borough surveyor of Bury for neatly twelve years, has been selected for the surveyorship of St. Helens at a salary of £6OO a year. The vacancy was caused by the retirement of Mr. 0. 3. C. Broom, who had been surveyor since ISSI. ONE RAILWAYMEN'S UNION.

Mr. VS'. Hudson, M.P. for Newcastle, was the principal speaker at a railway workers' meeting held at St. Helens on Sunday in support of a universal eight., hour day for railwaymen. He said that they were on the eve of a great reform, provided that the railwaymen of the country were true to themselves. They must look for a unity of force, and he wanted to ask them whether they believed in that.-IYea.) They could not play fast and loose in these matters ; they were bound to be serious, and if that expression of opinion which was cheered to the echo by all sections of railwaymen was real, what was the answer to-day ? The only answer was one union for railwaymen—(applause),—and that the sooner they got to work the better. He had heard some people talk about federation. Federation was very nice in theory, but it did not work out in practice. They had tried it, and they wanted something more something that was real unity. It was nothing short of a tragedy that over 100,000 men in the railway service were getting less than £1 a week.—(Sbame.) It was a shame, but it was a shame upon them to allow it to continue. The time was ripe for a stnndard working day for railway men as a whole, but they would not get it without a fight. Mr. Hudson mentioned that with a standard day 60,000 more men would be required on the present basis to work the lines of the country.— (Applause).

LABOUR CANDIDATE FOR ST. HELENS.

The St. Helens Labour party held a large meeting at the Town Hall on Ssturday night, when Mr. T. Glover, J.l', ex-M.P., was again adopted as the Labour cendi- date for the borough. Mr. James Hart (chairman of the Trades and Labour Council) presided, and there were present Mr. Glover, Mr. Stephen Walsh, M.P., and Messrs. W. C. Robiessis (National Labour Party), W. Forshaw, J. Francis, I'. Hamblett, J.P., A. Rudd, J.P., P. Phythian, and A. Valentine. ' Mr. James Hewitt presented a large framed illumin- ated address to Mr. Glover, stating :—" You have had the honour of worthily representing this borough in Parliament during a period remarkable for the rapid growth of a workers' party which seeks to sweeten aid enrich the lives of the toilers. We believe the worker• in this borough never before were no well represented. We are glad to know that you will again contest this borough at the first opportunity, and we hope that you will again be triumphantly returned and that you will long live t 3 be its esteemed member of Parliament.' Mr. Glover said he stood before them as a Labour representative pure and simple to ask them t 3 follow their own party, and not to be lei away by ei h Liberal or Tory. Their party had done many things, and one great measure that they were especially keen about was the Right to Work Bill. That had been talked about along time, and, though It night &purer that very little had been done, they had created a feel- ing in the minds of many Liberals and Tories that the state of the man who was out of work must be dealt with, and that in this particular things must le improved by some means or other. Though the Govern- ment did not appear very willing to take it up, the Labour party would press on until the question was dealt with. Thousands of working men in that and all other towns were working for Ifs., 17a. or 19s. per week. It was impossible for people to live and to do their duties to their families on such wages. The cry of the bottom dog was heard in the land. The wages of the workers were not increasing, but their food had increased in price by 20 and 30 per cent. The unrest siould not cease until every working man, whatever he might be working at, received no less than 21s. a week. Mr. Stephen Walsh, 31.1'., said that if they but possessed the will, they potioeesed power to make a clean sweep of many of the things which stood between them and their right as human beings, equal before God with the richest end proudest in the lend.

SPECIAL CONSTABLE CORPS DISBANDED. At Runcorn Sessions, on Monday, twenty-one special constables, who were sworn in during the recent railway strike, were discharged. Then:airman Dr M`Dougall) said the county end petty sessional divisional of Runcorn were deeply indebted to those people for coming forward at such a time, when there was tronblo in various parts of the country, but which, fortunately, did not touch Runcorn. lie had a special knowledge of Runcorn during the past forty-three years, and he was quite conscious that if the trouble did spread to Runcorn there would be no need for the special constables, because he knew too well how anxious they all were in Runcorn to keep law and order. " LANCASHIRE RAILWAY SOCIETY OF FREE WORKERS." NEW ORGANISATION AT WIDNES.

At Widnes on Monday, a meeting of non-union rail- waymen was held to protest against the tyranny of trade unions.

The principal speaker was Mr. Mark Judge, London, who contended that the time was come when trade unionists should be taught to behave themselves and non.unionists should combine against the forces of State Socialism with which trades unionism was synonymous. He described the letter of Mr. Williams, secretary of 'the A.S.R.S., as the most miserable and dastardly ever published in a free country. What was wanted was a counter organisation of non.unionists. A resolution was carried, with alight opposition, slating that the continued attempts of coercion on the part of trade unions made it imperative for workers who desire to remain free to organise in self-defence. and resolving to establish a society called the" Lanca- shire Railway Society of Free Worksrs," for the purpose of maintaining the principles of personol responsibility, and especially to protect the rights of individual members to live and work in freedom. Thirty joined the society.

THE WEEK'S HOBBY.

A DATE-CARD CASE.

From the following directions, the average amateur can make a very handy, and at the same time ornamental, date-card case. The wood used in its manufacture should be tin. thick. For the back (a) a piece bin. by Sin. will suffice. With a pair of compasses the correct shape is marked, and a fret-saw, carefully manipulated. does the rest. Pieces 3tin. by lin. are used for the sides, I a. Before shaping these, the unshaded part should be reduced in thickness slightly for purposes explained later. Then the oblong holes shown should be carefully made by

in., and sin. from the bottom. after which ehspe them. A piece 3in. by lin. is sufficient for the bottom, c. Great care should be exercised with regard to the holes at either end and the semicircular indention at the back. _ _

PUTTING THE PIECES TOGETHER.

After the above parts have been nicely sandpapered, etc.. proceed to put them to- gether. c should be fitted to B B by putting its ends through the holes in B B. They are held together by means of small neat wedges inserted in the holes in c which protrude on the outer side of B B. Now place the longer sides of B B within the dotted lines shown on a, and securely fasten with snvall screws driven through the back, and the frame is complete. Oct some clean cardboard, not toe thick, and the same on both sides. Cut fif- teen pieces, 21 in. by l in. in size, and

neatly print numbers 1 to 31, one on each side of each card for the days of the month; three pieces, 21 in. by 2 in., with the names of the months, one at each end of each side of each; and two pieces, 21 in. by 2,1 in., with the names of the days of the week. Let the edges of the cards be clean-cut. The tallest cards, giving the days of the week, should be put at the back, those giving the names of the months next, and the cards indicating the days of the month in front. The greater thickness of the sides in front keeps the cards from falling out.

A TERRIER'S KENNEL.

It is also easy to make a terrier's kennel. The materials required are as follows: 38 ft. of i-in. wood, 11 in. broad: i doz. 2-in. nails; 3 doz. 1-in, nails: 2 doz. 1-in. screws. The floor is the first consideration. Cut two pieces from the plank 2 ft. llf in. long, and two pieces 20 in. long by 51 in. broad; plans thent nicely 'on all sides. These pieces are now to be screwed together as shown in Fig. 1. Cut off a piece 3 ft. 9 in. long, and cut it through the central faint at an angle of 45

degrees. Place the long edges together to form the end of the kennel, and natl to the bottom with 2-in. nails. Make the front in the same way. but cut out a piece for the doorway. For the sides cut twelve pieces 18 in. long and 5 in. wide, and nail them to. gether—six on each side—as shown in the second illustration. The cross-bar, A, is 1 in. square Cut eight pieces 18 in. long, for the top, and nail them on. Let the edges hip about in. over the end. The kennel is now completed, and only needs painting or coal- HUGE STORAGE TARES.

LABOUR LEAGUE CLOSES ITS CAMPAIGN.

How the Foreigner Taxes Himself.

The Warrington Progressive Labour League brought another remarkably successful open-air campaign to • close on Monday evening with a meeting at Bridge Foot. A large and enthusiastic audience had the privilege of hearing a very able and destructive criticism of Protection by Miss Edith Hammond, of London, one of the foremost lady speakers on the Free Trade platform. Mr. J. Morton presided and was accompanied on the platform by Mr. and Mrs. E. Wynne Jones, Mr. T. R. Ashworth and Mr. H. lhorsas. The CHAIRMAN said that although Free Trade had been so frequently discussed at their meetings, it wss such a great and vital question that it could always be dealt with from a new point of view. Miss HAMMOND delivered an interesting speech full of sound argument and concrete instances of the evils of tariffs in foreign countries. She at once addressed herself to a tariff " reform " leaflet which declared " Our present system taxes you. Tariff reform will tax the foreigner." Having demonstrated the superior stability of Free Trade finance as shown by the Budgets of various countries, MiEfl Hammond asked whether, BS a logical people not carried away by violent impulses, we considered there was any argument for a change in oor policy when we found rival nations—France, Russia, Germany and America— each of whom had the power to tax the foreigner, showing a deficit instead of a surplus when they came to the end of their financial year? About twelve months ago the " Daily Mail " published a statement I to the effect that GF.RMANV'S ARMY AM, NAVY were largely paid for by taxes on English goods. That, the article added, applied to the navy and army of almost every country in the world. "It is ire" the "Daily Mail " said " who have hitherto paid for Germany's social reform. Let Germany now pay for a little of ours " and to-day they were telling us that the German insurance scheme was financed out of the duties on English manufactured goods. On going into the question, she discovered that in the year under notice the Germans spent 4.40,000,000 upon their army end £15,500,000 upon their Navy. Another little fact which would be rather startling to the tariff reformer was that the Germans collected in duties on manufactured goods at their ports a paltry £7,ooo,ooo—not from England alone but from the whole world. (Applause) Therefore, the pro- ceeds of the tariff on manufactured goods went about , one-sixth of the way to providing the German Army ' —let alone the Navy. She was forced to ths con- clusion that for once the " Daily Mail " had made a slight error. (Laughter.) We in this country provided our Dreadnoughts out of current revenue. The Germans, with their power to tax the foreigner—if it could be done—got theirs on "tick.' (Laughter.) They raised a loan of between £40,000,000 and £50,000,000 to pay for them. At the end of 1908 they were £25,000,000 short. How did they raise that sum ? People who believed in the promise that under Protection we could shift our burdens on to somebody else, would have an idea that the Germans would make us or the French- men pay that deficit. But instead of doing that, they levied taxeu on beer, tobacco, coffee, tea, spirits and matches. She had been told that the price of matchts was now so high in Germany that some men could not afford to smoke at all on windy nights. (Laughter). Electricity was also taxed—a curious way of encourag- ing industries—and there were increased levies on sugar, coal, property transfers, bills of exchange and even on railway tickets. Was not the latter impost one that we should particularly resent in this country ? The Conservative" Morning Post "bud an article in June last in which it &teenaged the

RAILWAY TICKET TA( and its difficulties. It said that the railway companies in Germany were beginning to complain because the people who used to travel first now travelled second, and so on down to the fourth class. We in England did not want a fourth class, but in Protected Germany, where they were all so wealthy, they liked to travel fourth. (Laughter.) The railway companies were suggesting that the Government should pat a tax on the fourth class to drive the people op again. (laughter.) The people were objecting and the "Morning Post " ended with the very significant words that the German Government would be willing to give up the duty on railway tickets, as it was praying the cause of so much dissEnsion, but they did not know where to turn to raise the £1,00,000 which the tax brought in. And that in spite of their power to tax the foreigner. (Applause). The thirteenth yearly Conference of the Lancashir' Congregational Union Lay Preachers' Association We held on Saturday afternoon in Chapel-street Congregational Church, Southport,under the presidency of Mr. Benjamin Aspinall, of Orcell, Wigan. Mr. William Woof, of Askew, who has been a lay preacher for fifty years, was elector chairman. Mr. George Nicholls, of Peterborough, ex-Libetal .M.P. for North Northants, gave an address, in which he said Oust as long as people felt they could never go into God's house unless thy had what we were pleased to call "decent clothes," he felt that ministers somehow had not convinced the people that Jesus Christ wag once a working man. He never felt no much reproach es on a Sunday morning, when he passed a Roman Catholic Church, for the worshippers there %fart in their clogs and working clothes and shawls. In the evening a public meeting was held, presided over by Mr. B. Aspinall, of Orrell. He observed that the material aspect of the Sermoo on the Mount and other portions of Christ's teaching were possibly unduly pressed to the front, and that sometimes at the expense of the moral and spiritual. The Rev. Fred Hibbert, president of the Lancashire Congregational Union, and Mr. George Nicholls also spoke. The latter, as a by preacher, appealed to his colleagues not to be always rubbing in the drink enemy as though that was the only one. No man had a right to trust Parliament to redeem mankind. The State, when it did its beat, could only offer facilities for improvement. The work of the ssint was as much to lift up as to look sip. They had to get in touch wi.h the weakling and the wastrel. If all the failures of the State were rectified to.mwrow, if they filled West- minster with Lab Air members, the individual would still be his own social saviuur.

Races.

A crowded and enthusiastic audience testified 'to the reinarkab!e interest now taken in this healthy and invaluable exercise, while a highly encouraging feature of the races was the excellent array of youthful talent which they brought together. Who knows but that Warrington may (as the Mayor ventured to express the hope), have the honour of claimirg come future Channel-conqueror as a product of the go d o!d town "! There were no open events but the programme did not suffer to any serious extent on that account, as the various cham,donships of the town were the object of strenuous struggles. The 150 yards race did not however, call forth as large an entry as might reasonably have been expected. It was good to see the zest is itli which the schoolboys entered into their special event.
