THE MAYOR'S ADVICE.

During an inte real the Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph), who presided, gave a short address. He said he had a greet admiration for swimming which was quite different trout soy other forms of muscular exercise. Man Wale not naturally an aquatic baler...be was not built that way—but after witnessing the con- tests that evening he (the Mayor) felt that, after all, youths and girls were jest as well adapted for swimming as frogs were. Referring to the physical advise tg tea of swimming, his worship pointed out that not only were the muscles brought into play in a special way but their movement in the water must have a very great effect in developing them. On that account swimming ought to be greatly encouraged and he was glad to see it no well patronized by the younger generation in Warrington, as well as by the parents wh3 assembled in such large numbers at the gates. He could not help at a gather- ing of that character alluding to the achievement of Burgess, who had performed such a grand feat in crossing the Channel. (Loud at plans...) It was a matter for great congratulation that after the lapse of thirty-nix years since Captain Webb's tremendous feat and after many unsuccessful attempts it still fell to the lot of one of our countrymen to achieve that splendid distinction. (Applause). It was particularly fitting that the representative of • maritime nation and the greatest naval power in the world should have con- quered Father Neptune and crossed the Channel. (Applause He would not be much surprised if one I of the young swimmers who had taken pert in that evening's contest tezame a future Burgess. (Applause.) Baths, continued the Mayor, were very excellent institution bit they might be abused In the remarks be now wished to make he was

not alluding to the campetit inithat gala, as it could be t tken for granted that expert swimmers acre healthy and clam and It to mix with where. It might be, however, that some who entered a public bath like that were not exactly fit to di no. It was the bounden duty of every bather te:ensure that before making um of a public I ath he we fairly clean, so that he would not interfere with the comfort or endanger the health of thope who were participating in the same txercise. , Some people might think it was faddy to emphasise strh a point, but the more he considered some so called fads, he was forced to the conolusion that there was something in them. He was sure it was of great importance that lathers should be careful in the respect he had mentioned. Tao best say to ensure that was for people to be deal in their own homes. To use a parallel, jat as people who went to church every Sunday ought also to be particular about their ordinary lives during the week, ea people who were not clean in their homes had no right to come to the baths and mix with other people so as to endanger them. In some Continental places there was a kind of examination of swimmers and bathers and they had to mtske use of a shower bath before entering the large public bath. That wag a good plan but the best method was for the people generally to be clean in their own homes. There was plenty of water for everybody—rich and poor—and s tap was not very dear, so be thought they bad a right to expect cleanliness. (Applause.) lie hoped the time would come when the bathers In Warrington would have still better •ccommodttion. (Applause). REGIMENTAL COLOURS FOR THE PARISH CHURCH.

Forthcoming Military Ceremonial.

Impressive military ceremonial will take place at Warrington on Friday, October oth, when the old colours of the 2c.d Battalion Prince of Wales Volunteers &lath Lancashire Regiment, will be presented to the Rector of Warrington at the Parish Church. The battalion, which is at present stationed in Ire!and, was presented with new colours by His Majesty the King, on the occasion of his recent visit to that country, and it is fitting that the old colours should be deposited in the ancient church of this town with which the South Lancashire Regiment has such close associations. The presentation of the colours will be marked by a special prude in which the kcal Tem itorials will may a prominent part. In a special order to the battalion, it is stated : The Commanding Officer hopes that on this most impressive occasion all ranks will make a special effort to attend. The battalion has been invited to supply torch bearers as escort on each side of the colours. The rarty will parade in service.dresalhaki. The remainder of the battalion will parade in scarlet. N.C.O.'s and men desirou, of acting as torch bearers must enter their names on the rolls provided in the Drill Hall. A procession will be formed as follows

Band of 4th Battalion P.W. Volunteers, South Lancashire Regiment. Lieut -Colonel J. I). Fairclough, T.D., and half battalion 4'h P.W.V. South Lancashire Regt. Half number 3 Company Army Service Corps, W.L.T.D. Band of Bth and 4,/th Regimental Dept. Mayor of Warrington and civic officials. Guard of honour from the depot, South Lancashire Reg.ment. OLYMPIC RAMMED BY CRUISER.

WARRINGTON PASSENGER ON

BOARD.

Exciting Experience.

An alarming collision, but without any injury or loss of life, took place on Wednes- day afternoon off Cowes between the White Star liner Olympic and the first-class pro- tected cruiser Hawke. The Olympic—which is the largest vessel afloat—was on her outward voyage to New York containing a record number of first-class passengers and over 20 "millionaires."

The collision took place just after the Olympic had turned on her eastward course on leaving Southampton Water. The Hawke overtook her at a greater speed, and it is suggested by those who saw the collision from the shore that it was caused by the cruiser altering her course when close along- side the liner. EJ Collision mats were got out, and the Olympic remained at anchor in Cowes-roads. The Hawke put into Portsmouth under her own steam. Some of the passengers landed lon Wednesday night at Southampton.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
A 20-FEET HOLE.

When the accident happened there was a terrific crash, the sound of which was heard on the shore almost like a clap of thunder. It was all too evident what had happened. The Hawke's bow had crashed into the Olympic's starboard quarter, making a huge hole some 20ft. across, and extending some distance below the water line.

For some time the greatest excitement prevailed on board the liner; but the passengers kept wonderfully cool. The warship was almost worse damaged, her stem being smashed and twisted for about 20ft. both above and below the water- line.

===== 0002601_19110923_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
A WARRINGTON PASSENGER.

Mr. H. M. Ashton, chemist, of 63, Sankey- street, Warrington, was one of the many passengers on the giant steamer, and sends an interesting, if hurriedly penned, account of the disaster, which we print below. Mr. Ashton, in company with a brother and a friend, was proceeding as far as Cherbourg in the Olympic„at the outset for a holiday in France, and was greatly looking forward to the short voyage on the famous liner. Mr. Ashton writes :

On Board R.M.S. Olympic. Wednesday, 2.5 p.m. _

-„ . . . We boarded this fine vessel at 10 o'clock this morning. One could not view it from the landing-stage without exclaim- ing at its marvellous proportions, and once on board our wonder increased.

It seems almost incredible that such a structure could possibly float safely on the sea; huge smoking-rooms, palatial dining- rooms upholstered in magnificent style, a racque, court, swimming bath, skating rink, and all the thousand and one attractions and conveniences of a huge modern hotel. The time soon passed in our tour of inspec- tion and at 12.30 we were sitting down to a comfortable luncheon. About one o'clock we were passing pretty close to a second-class cruiser when suddenly we felt a slight shock, and a mild surprise was manifested by the diners, but there was absolutely no excite- ment. Waiters hurried to and fro and people continued their mastication. But it was soon ascertained that the cruiser in turning, as we were passing, had rammed our stern and crashed into a second-class cabin whose owner had only a moment before vacated it to take his place at lunch. Then the vessel came to a standstill, and I was able, with my brother and a friend, to view for myself the damage done. By climbing up to a porthole above the damaged portion we discovered a huge rent about 16 feet long and only ending about a foot above the water-line. No danger certainly! but the pumps were going hard and all our water-tight compartments have been closed in the quarters. We are now on the way back to Southampton, and shall probably get a night boat to Cherbourg but I am sorry for the hundreds of Americans on their way home, some of whom have waited weeks for a berth and will now be delayed still further. The cruiser is lying in sulky silence at some distance with its nose all buckled and bent. I don't suppose that £50,000 will cover the damage done considering the delay. transference of goods and loss of passage money that will ensue.

Yoii will have heard the news, no doubt, before you get this note. It is my first experience of a collision at sea, and it's not one of the pleasantest. I wish I could send you a photograph of the rent, but aq I write I see a small motor-boat rushing alongside with two camera-fiends in it. The marconigraph has been busy ever since the accident."

===== 0002601_19110923_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====

WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

THE PILE DRIVING CEREMONY.

The engineer of the new Warrington Bridge has arranged for the driving of two piles on Thursday, October sth, at 3.15 p.m. The Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph) and Alderman Smethurst (chairman of thc- Bridges Committee) will perform the ceremony. The piles will be driven on the Cheshire side of the river. A luncheon will be held in the Parr Hall at 1.30. The price of tickets is ss. each. exclusive of wines.
